# ÂµT-Kernel 3.0 on RP2040 - feature status

Dual-core **SMP** port of ÂµT-Kernel 3.0 to the Raspberry Pi Pico W (RP2040, two
Cortex-M0+ cores), derived from the official ÂµT-Kernel 3.0 BSP Pico port. This
is the only SMP port in the family - the ESP32-C3 and ESP32-C6 ports are
single-core.

This document states what works, what is implemented but not proven, and what
is absent. Each entry is one of three states:

- **Validated** - implemented and confirmed on real hardware, with the result recorded below.
- **Ported, not validated** - implemented and believed working, but without a rigorous or long-running hardware test behind it. Use with that in mind.
- **Not ported** - absent from this port. Calling it will fail, or the feature simply does not exist.

Validation dates and the profile used are given where they are known. The
phase harnesses and the per-phase hardware logs that produced these results
are not part of this release; see
[Performance numbers](../README.md#performance-numbers) in the README.

> [!IMPORTANT]
> "Qualified" in this port means a profile passed its full phase gate on
> hardware. The qualified boundary is **SMP + UART**, plus an optional
> **SMP + USB-CDC** console profile. Everything outside that boundary -
> notably the radio and arbitrary concurrent peripheral access - is a
> development profile, however far along it is.

---

## Validated

### SMP kernel core

Qualified through Phase 12: **SMP 58/58**, single-core regression **11/11**.

| Feature | Notes |
|---|---|
| Two-core symmetric scheduling | Global ready queue with per-core dispatch. Phase 6 (24/24) and Phase 7 (27/27). |
| Per-core kernel state | Separate per-core scheduler bookkeeping; Phase 2 (7/7) on both profiles. |
| Core 1 bring-up and IPI | Second core started and driven by inter-processor interrupt; Phase 3 (12/12). |
| Recursive big kernel lock | Built on RP2040 hardware spinlocks and atomics; Phase 4 (18/18) and Phase 5 (22/22). |
| Kernel objects under SMP | Semaphores, event flags, mutexes, mailboxes, message buffers, memory pools all serialized across cores; Phase 8 (34/34), both profiles. |
| Time management | Core 0 is the sole SysTick timekeeper; core 1 disables its local SysTick and the handler rejects and counts any off-core tick. Cyclic/alarm handlers support `TA_ASSPRC` processor assignment, with remote callbacks delivered by a coalesced IPI. Phase 9 (40/40). |
| Remote task management | Task operations targeting a task resident on the other core; Phase 10 (50/50). |
| Task affinity | Static assignment of a task to a processor. |
| Lock behaviour under load | 23,470 contentions at the SMP benchmark, 36 Âµs maximum wait, 85 Âµs maximum hold, zero illegal unlocks. |
| Ten-second SMP soak | 501 ownership audits, 3,785,087 core-1 lock operations, zero invariant failures. |

### Measured latency

64-sample runs on the qualified profiles.

| Path | min / avg / max |
|---|---|
| SMP local wake and dispatch | 16 / 16 / 43 Âµs |
| SMP cross-core wake | 17 / 17 / 24 Âµs |
| Single-core local wake and dispatch | 2 / 3 / 31 Âµs |

Idle stack high water: SMP 244/1024 bytes on core 0 and 108/1024 on core 1;
single-core 108/256 bytes.

These are observed measurements and broad bounded-response gates, **not
hard-real-time guarantees**. The recursive big kernel lock masks local
interrupts while held, so workload-specific worst-case latency still needs
application-level analysis.

### SMP scaling

Phase 13 (**65/65**) adds no kernel code and does not broaden the release
boundary. With two tasks and a 384-job workload: both workers pinned to
processor 1 took a 1,383,657 Âµs median; one worker per processor took
768,751 Âµs - **1.799x throughput, 44.4% less elapsed time**. All six trials
matched fixed golden checksums with zero API errors.

### Console

| Feature | Notes |
|---|---|
| UART console | UART0, GP0 TX / GP1 RX, 115200 8N1. The qualified console. |
| USB-CDC console | Optional qualified profile, Phase 11 (**71/71**). Dual-core print storm: two pinned tasks issued 64 framed `tm_printf` calls each from opposite processors; the host transcript contained all 128 frames, the device observed processor mask `3`, the whole-call lock recorded 111 contentions, and the ring reported zero unintended drops and a successful drain. Completed in 475,151 Âµs including the synchronous UART mirror. |

A later intentional overflow recorded 5,155 dropped bytes and kept running -
those drops are expected and excluded from the no-loss storm gate.

### Radio bring-up (development profile, not in the qualified release)

`make SMP=1 WIFI=cyw43` passed its **72/72** Pico W hardware gate: CYW43439
firmware boot, station interface enable, OTP MAC retrieval, and active scan,
with USB-CDC coexistence. A processor-1-owned polling task drives the radio.

A **single-core** radio build (`SMP=0 CONSOLE=usb_cdc WIFI=cyw43 WIFI_JOIN=1
WIFI_NETIF=1 WIFI_DHCP=1 WIFI_DNS=1 WIFI_UDP=1`) was subsequently confirmed on
hardware: association, DHCP, and a full 64-packet UDP echo exchange against a
Raspberry Pi Zero 2 W - `sent=64 recv=64 matched=64/64 corrupt=0 retries=0
errs=0` in 1400 ms, with the host counting the same 64 datagrams. The
single-core equivalents of the SMP primitives are in
`lib/libwifi/sysdepend/pico_rp2040/smp_compat.h`.

The first run on 2026-08-16 preserved every kernel/SMP invariant but exposed
that PIO0/PIO1 and DMA had not been released from reset, so PIO-SPI bring-up
failed; the corrected run passed.

---

## Ported, not validated

| Feature | What is missing |
|---|---|
| IP networking over CYW43439 | lwIP-based DHCP, static addressing, DNS, UDP and TCP profiles exist as cumulative `WIFI_*` build knobs. Association, DHCP and the repeating 64-packet UDP echo have run end to end on hardware in **both core counts**, matching 64/64 with the host's own count across repeated sessions; static addressing and the TCP paths have had less exposure. None of it is part of the qualified release - treat as development work. |
| Inherited peripheral drivers under SMP | UART, I2C and ADC paths were converted from local interrupt masking to SMP-aware serialization - atomic mSDI `FastLock`, interrupt-preserving cross-core locks, each peripheral IRQ permanently owned by processor 1 / RP2040 core 0, auto-cleared event flags replacing sleep/wakeup handoffs. Both profiles compile and link, but **only** the monitor USB transmit path is qualified for concurrent cross-core use. |
| Long-run stability | The longest recorded soak is ten seconds. Behaviour over hours or days is uncharacterised. |

---

## Not ported

| Feature | Status |
|---|---|
| Arbitrary concurrent peripheral access | Cross-core concurrent use of the inherited UART, I2C, ADC, DMA, PWM or other drivers is outside the qualified boundary. Phase 11 qualifies only the monitor USB transmit path; treat every other shared block as single-owner. |
| Application protocols over lwIP | None ship. The transports (UDP, TCP, TCP bulk) are present and working; anything layered on top - MQTT, HTTP, CoAP - is left as an exercise. lwIP's own `apps/` sources are available in the Pico SDK checkout if you want to build against them, and `LWIP_ALTCP` in `lib/libnet/lwip/include/lwipopts.h` is the switch some of them need. |
| Rendezvous ports | `CNF_MAX_PORID=0` - compiled out. |
| mT-Kernel/DS debug support | Not present in this configuration. |
| SMP-safe flash erase/program | Not implemented. |
| Dynamic affinity | Task-to-processor assignment is static. |
| Partitioned scheduling, EDF | Not implemented. |
| Memory protection, execution budgets, temporal/fault isolation | Not implemented. |
| On-board LED | The Pico W's LED is on the CYW43439 radio, not an RP2040 pin, so the liveness indicator is an external LED on `BOARD_LED_PIN` (GP16 by default, in `include/sys/sysdepend/pico_rp2040/sysdef.h`). GP23, GP24, GP25 and GP29 are reserved by the radio. |

---

## Non-goals

These are deliberate constraints, not gaps.

- **No FPU.** The RP2040's Cortex-M0+ cores have no floating-point unit.
- **Exactly two processors.** The port assumes the RP2040's two cores; it does not generalise to more.
- **Not hard real-time.** The big kernel lock masks local interrupts while held; the measured figures are bounded-response gates, not guarantees.

---

## RP2040 safety rules

These are part of the qualification boundary, not optional tuning advice.

1. **SIO hardware spinlocks 0-2 are kernel-reserved** - 0 for kernel-lock word transitions, 1 for architecture atomics, 2 for IPI pending masks. Applications and imported SDK code must not claim them.
2. **A shared peripheral IRQ must have one owner core**, unless its driver is explicitly designed and locked for delivery to both NVICs. SysTick runs only on core 0. Each core owns its corresponding SIO FIFO IRQ for IPIs.
3. **Publish shared data before making another core observe** the associated ready flag, queue entry, or IPI. Use the port's atomic/barrier primitives; `volatile` alone is not inter-core synchronization.
4. **Never rebuild, free, or reuse a remotely running task's stack** until its processor has completed the final context save and released ownership.
5. **Any flash operation that disables or disrupts XIP must first park the other core** in SRAM-safe code. Flash erase/program was not exercised by this qualification and must not be added without that protocol.
6. **Treat serial, I2C, ADC, DMA, PWM and other shared blocks as single-owner resources** unless an SMP-safe driver supplies its own serialization and interrupt-ownership policy. The UART qualification covers the synchronous monitor path used by this release, not arbitrary concurrent driver use.
7. **Preserve the RP2040 core-1 NMI-mask/watchdog caution** from the datasheet: do not assume core 1 has the same NMI behaviour as core 0, and coordinate watchdog/reset actions across both processors.
8. **On Pico W the CYW43439 remains powered down** in the qualified profiles. GP23, GP24, GP25 and GP29 are radio-connected and reserved; the external liveness LED defaults to GP16.
9. **TinyUSB state and USBCTRL interrupt delivery are owned by processor 1** (physical core 0). Producers on either processor may use `tm_printf`, but must not call TinyUSB directly. The whole-call console lock masks local interrupts, making `tm_printf` diagnostic output rather than hard-real-time.

---

## Driver and resource ownership

"Processor 1" is the ÂµT-Kernel processor number returned by `tk_get_prc()`; it
is physical RP2040 core 0.

| Resource | Owner | Required serialization |
|---|---|---|
| USBCTRL IRQ and TinyUSB device state | Processor 1 | Service task pinned `TP_PRC1`; USBCTRL enabled only in core 0's NVIC |
| USB transmit ring | Consumed by the USB service; produced from either processor | Whole `tm_printf` call lock, then the interrupt-preserving ring lock |
| UART0 T-Monitor mirror | Processor 1 owns configuration and direct access | The same whole-call lock serializes the UART mirror |
| Sample UART / I2C / ADC driver IRQs | Processor 1 | Atomic mSDI lock serializes requests; per-unit interrupt-preserving spinlock protects task/IRQ handoff |
| CYW43439 PIO-SPI, DMA, pins and state | Processor 1 | Service task pinned `TP_PRC1`; other tasks may only read its published status |
| SysTick / timekeeper | Processor 1 | Core 1 SysTick remains disabled |
| SIO FIFO IRQs | The corresponding processor | Core 0 owns `SIO_IRQ_PROC0`; core 1 owns `SIO_IRQ_PROC1` |
| Flash / XIP control | System-wide | Other core must complete an acknowledged SRAM-safe park protocol first |
| Watchdog / reset | System-wide | One designated control task; coordinate both processors |

---

## Idle and exception model

Each processor owns a private idle TCB and stack, outside the public task
table and ready queue. An empty processor restores that context normally and
issues `WFI` in Thread mode. SysTick and PendSV share the lowest implemented
priority (`SHPR3 = c0c00000`), so SysTick cannot interrupt a half-saved
context.

Core 0 is the sole timekeeper. Core 1 disables its local SysTick and is woken
for runnable work by the coalesced SIO FIFO IPI transport.

---

/*
 * Copyright (c) 2020 Raspberry Pi (Trading) Ltd.
 *
 * SPDX-License-Identifier: BSD-3-Clause
 */

#ifndef _BOOT_UF2_H
#define _BOOT_UF2_H

#include <stdint.h>
#include <assert.h>

/** \file uf2.h
*  \defgroup boot_uf2_headers boot_uf2_headers
*
* \brief Header file for the UF2 format supported by a RP2xxx chip in BOOTSEL mode
*/

#define UF2_MAGIC_START0 0x0A324655u
#define UF2_MAGIC_START1 0x9E5D5157u
#define UF2_MAGIC_END    0x0AB16F30u

#define UF2_FLAG_NOT_MAIN_FLASH          0x00000001u
#define UF2_FLAG_FILE_CONTAINER          0x00001000u
#define UF2_FLAG_FAMILY_ID_PRESENT       0x00002000u
#define UF2_FLAG_MD5_PRESENT             0x00004000u
#define UF2_FLAG_EXTENSION_FLAGS_PRESENT 0x00008000u

// Extra family IDs
#define CYW43_FIRMWARE_FAMILY_ID    0xe48bff55u

// Bootrom supported family IDs
#define RP2040_FAMILY_ID            0xe48bff56u
#define ABSOLUTE_FAMILY_ID          0xe48bff57u
#define DATA_FAMILY_ID              0xe48bff58u
#define RP2350_ARM_S_FAMILY_ID      0xe48bff59u
#define RP2350_RISCV_FAMILY_ID      0xe48bff5au
#define RP2350_ARM_NS_FAMILY_ID     0xe48bff5bu
#define BOOTROM_FAMILY_ID_MIN       RP2040_FAMILY_ID
#define BOOTROM_FAMILY_ID_MAX       RP2350_ARM_NS_FAMILY_ID

// Defined for backwards compatibility
#define FAMILY_ID_MAX               BOOTROM_FAMILY_ID_MAX

// 04 e3 57 99
#define UF2_EXTENSION_RP2_IGNORE_BLOCK 0x9957e304

struct uf2_block {
    // 32 byte header
    uint32_t magic_start0;
    uint32_t magic_start1;
    uint32_t flags;
    uint32_t target_addr;
    uint32_t payload_size;
    uint32_t block_no;
    uint32_t num_blocks;
    uint32_t file_size; // or familyID;
    uint8_t  data[476];
    uint32_t magic_end;
};

static_assert(sizeof(struct uf2_block) == 512, "uf2_block not sector sized");

#endif

    uint32_t    sh_offset;

    uint16_t    ph_num;
    uint16_t    sh_entry_size;
    uint16_t    sh_num;
    uint16_t    sh_str_index;

    uint32_t vaddr;
    uint32_t paddr;

# ÂµT-Kernel 3.0 - RP2040 dual-core SMP Port

ÂµT-Kernel 3.0 (IEEE 2050-2018) on the Raspberry Pi Pico W (RP2040, two Cortex-M0+ cores), derived from the official ÂµT-Kernel 3.0 BSP Pico port. It builds from one source tree in two qualified configurations: **single core** (the default) and **dual-core SMP**, where both cores run the same kernel against a global ready queue serialized by a recursive big kernel lock built on RP2040 hardware spinlocks.

```sh
make          # single core  (default)
make SMP=1    # dual-core SMP
```

**Current capabilities**

- **Single core by default, SMP when you ask for it** - one `SMP` switch, one source tree; both configurations pass their own hardware gate (single core 11/11, SMP 58/58)
- **True SMP scheduling** - global ready queue with per-core dispatch, static task affinity, cross-core wake, inter-processor interrupts, and remote task management, all qualified on hardware
- **Full preemptive kernel** - tasks, priorities, semaphores, event flags, mutexes, mailboxes, message buffers, fixed and variable memory pools, cyclic and alarm handlers with per-processor assignment
- **Measured 1.799x speedup** - the same 384-job workload runs 44.4% faster with one worker per core than with both pinned to one
- **Two qualified console profiles** - UART0 (the baseline) and an optional SMP USB-CDC profile that survives a dual-core print storm with zero unintended drops
- **CYW43439 radio bring-up** - firmware boot, station enable, OTP MAC and active scan pass a 72/72 hardware gate as a development profile
- **Working IP networking** - DHCP, DNS and a repeating 64-packet UDP echo exchange, confirmed end to end against a Linux host on both one core and two

See [docs/PORT_RP2040.md](docs/PORT_RP2040.md) for exactly what is validated on hardware, what is ported but not yet validated, and what is not ported.

> [!IMPORTANT]
> The qualified release boundary is **SMP + UART**, plus the optional SMP
> USB-CDC console. IP networking and arbitrary concurrent peripheral access
> are development profiles, not part of that boundary.
> [docs/PORT_RP2040.md](docs/PORT_RP2040.md) states the supported scope and
> the RP2040 safety rules.

---

## Hardware

- Raspberry Pi **Pico W** (RP2040, dual Cortex-M0+, 2 MB flash)
- USB cable (data, not charge-only)
- A USB-serial adapter for the UART console - GP0 TX, GP1 RX, 115200 8N1
- An **external LED on GP16** for the liveness indicator. The Pico W's on-board LED is wired to the CYW43439 radio rather than to an RP2040 pin, so it is unavailable in the radio-down qualified profiles. GP23, GP24, GP25 and GP29 are reserved by the radio.

---

## Quick start

```sh
git clone https://github.com/sirfonzie/mtk3smp-rp2040.git
cd mtk3smp-rp2040

# build -- single core by default
cd build_make
make -j8
```

Hold **BOOTSEL** while connecting the Pico, then copy
`build_make/mtk3pico_smp0_uart.uf2` to the `RPI-RP2` drive that appears.

To build the dual-core SMP kernel instead, add `SMP=1` -- see
[Building for one core or two](#building-for-one-core-or-two).

Requires `arm-none-eabi-gcc` (baseline: 13.2.1) and a host `g++`. The build
compiles the ELF-to-UF2 converter itself on first run - the RP2040 bootloader
accepts UF2, not ELF, and unlike a Pico SDK/CMake project nothing else
provides that helper here. See
[docs/GETTING_STARTED.md](docs/GETTING_STARTED.md) for a full step-by-step
walkthrough.

| Doc | Covers |

| [docs/GETTING_STARTED.md](docs/GETTING_STARTED.md) | Step-by-step toolchain setup, build, flash, console, troubleshooting |
| [docs/PORT_RP2040.md](docs/PORT_RP2040.md) | Feature status: what is validated on hardware, what is ported but not validated, and what is not ported. Also carries the RP2040 safety rules, the driver-ownership matrix, and the idle/exception model |

## Building for one core or two

**The build is single core by default.** `SMP` is the switch, and it is the
only thing that decides how many RP2040 cores the kernel uses:

| You want | Command | Produces |
|---|---|---|
| **One core** (default) | `make -j8` | `mtk3pico_smp0_uart.uf2` |
| **Two cores** (SMP) | `make SMP=1 -j8` | `mtk3pico_smp1_uart.uf2` |

`make` with no arguments is exactly `make SMP=0`. Core 1 is never started, the
SMP scheduler is compiled out, and the kernel behaves as the stock
single-core ÂµT-Kernel 3.0 Pico BSP. This is the conservative choice and the
one to use if you are unsure.

`SMP=1` starts the second core, enables the global ready queue, the recursive
big kernel lock and the IPI transport, and lets tasks be assigned to either
processor. Both configurations are qualified â€” Phase 12 passed **58/58** in
SMP and **11/11** in the single-core regression â€” and both are built from the
same source tree.

Switching between profiles needs no manual cleanup: the build stamps a
profile id into `build_make/.build_profile` and discards objects built for a
different one automatically, reporting
`Build profile is now ...; discarding objects built for another profile.`

### Choosing the console

`CONSOLE` is independent of `SMP`:

| Console | Command | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| UART0 (default) | `make SMP=1 -j8` | GP0 TX / GP1 RX, 115200 8N1. Needs a USB-serial adapter. No external dependencies. |
| USB-CDC | `make SMP=1 CONSOLE=usb_cdc PICO_SDK_PATH=... -j8` | Console over the Pico's own USB port. Qualified for SMP at 71/71. **Requires the Pico SDK** - it links TinyUSB from it. |

The USB console is the only part of this tree that needs the Pico SDK, and it
uses just the TinyUSB library, not the SDK's CMake build. Point
`PICO_SDK_PATH` at your checkout, or export it once. Without it the build
stops with `TinyUSB not found at <path>. Set PICO_SDK_PATH, or build with
CONSOLE=uart`.

### All profiles at a glance

| Profile | Command | Status |
|---|---|---|
| Single core, UART | `make -j8` | **Qualified** â€” the default, 11/11 regression gate |
| Dual core, UART | `make SMP=1 -j8` | **Qualified** â€” the SMP baseline, 58/58 |
| Dual core, USB-CDC | `make SMP=1 CONSOLE=usb_cdc -j8` | **Qualified** â€” optional, 71/71 |
| Radio boot + active scan | `make SMP=1 WIFI=cyw43 -j8` | Development â€” 72/72 hardware gate, not in the release |

The artifact name always follows the profile:
`mtk3pico_smp<SMP>_<CONSOLE>[<wifi>].uf2`.

### Networking (development only)

Networking builds on the radio profile through additional knobs â€”
`WIFI_JOIN`, `WIFI_NETIF`, `WIFI_STATIC`, `WIFI_DHCP`, `WIFI_DNS`,
`WIFI_UDP`, `WIFI_TCP`, `WIFI_TCPBULK`. Each builds on the one before it
(`WIFI_UDP` needs `WIFI_DNS`, `WIFI_TCP` needs `WIFI_UDP`). These read local
configuration headers that are deliberately **not** committed; copy the
matching `config/*.example.h` and edit it:

```sh
cp config/wifi_credentials.example.h config/wifi_credentials.h
cp config/network_config.example.h   config/network_config.h
# fill in the values, and set each file's *_SET flag from 0 to 1
make SMP=1 WIFI=cyw43 WIFI_JOIN=1 WIFI_NETIF=1 WIFI_DHCP=1 -j8
```

Each template guards itself: an unedited copy fails at compile time with a
message naming the file and the flag, rather than building and then failing
to associate at runtime.

`config/wifi_credentials.h`, `network_config.h`, `udp_test_config.h`,
and `tcp_test_config.h` are all gitignored. Never commit
a populated copy.

## What the demo does (what you see on first flash)

A small **producer/consumer pipeline** across four tasks, putting one pass
through every IPC primitive the kernel offers. Radio builds add a fifth task
that reports WiFi status and the UDP echo traffic:

```
producer  --[fixed memory pool]--> record
          --[mutex]-------------> shared sequence counter
          --[message buffer]----> consumer
consumer  --[semaphore]---------> credit returned to producer
          --[event flag]--------> monitor woken
monitor   prints a status line
blink     LED liveness, independent of all of the above
```

Under SMP the producer is pinned to processor 1 and the consumer to processor
2 with `TA_ASSPRC`, so every message crosses a core boundary:

```
=== uT-Kernel 3.0 / RP2040 demo ===
SMP build: 2 processors
producer -> [mbf] -> consumer, 2 credits, 500 ms period

[monitor] seq=6 produced=6 consumed=6 dropped=0  made_on=prc1 seen_on=prc2
```

Built with `SMP=0` the same source runs unchanged on one core and both read
`prc1`. The blink task on GP16 is deliberately independent of the console: if
output stops but the LED still blinks, the console is at fault; if the LED
freezes, the kernel is.

It is one file with the tunables at the top - meant to be read, changed and
reflashed. **Full walkthrough**, including which call demonstrates which
object and what to try changing:
[docs/GETTING_STARTED.md Â§ 6b](docs/GETTING_STARTED.md#6b-what-the-demo-does-and-what-you-should-see).

---

## Performance numbers

The measured qualification results - wake/dispatch latency, cross-core wake,
lock contention and hold times, idle stack high water, and the Phase 13
scaling benchmark - are recorded in
[docs/PORT_RP2040.md](docs/PORT_RP2040.md), with the date and profile of each
run.

**The phase harnesses that produced them, and the per-phase hardware captures,
are not part of this release.** They are held back with the numbers being
prepared for publication, and will be published alongside them so the results
are reproducible from this repository.

The figures are observed measurements and broad bounded-response gates, **not
hard-real-time guarantees**. The recursive big kernel lock masks local
interrupts while held, so workload-specific worst-case latency still requires
application-level analysis.

```
device/            Device manager and inherited peripheral drivers
config/            Kernel configuration; *.example.h templates for local secrets

etc/linker/pico_rp2040/
                   Linker script

```

## Testing and Bug Reports

This port is qualified by phase gates run on real Pico W hardware. The
qualified boundary is SMP + UART (Phase 12: SMP 58/58, single-core 11/11),
plus the optional SMP USB-CDC console (Phase 11: 71/71). Phase 13 adds an
application-level scaling demonstration at 65/65.

**Soak testing note:** the longest recorded soak is **ten seconds** - 501
ownership audits and 3,785,087 core-1 lock operations with zero invariant
failures. Behaviour over hours or days has not been characterised. The
qualified profiles should be considered well-gated but not long-run proven.

Embedded software depends heavily on board revision, toolchain version, and
usage patterns that may not have been covered. If you hit unexpected
behaviour, a crash, or a reproducible failure, please open an issue with what
happened, how to reproduce it, the console output at the point of failure,
your `arm-none-eabi-gcc` version, and your board.

**Where help is most useful:** [docs/PORT_RP2040.md](docs/PORT_RP2040.md)
lists what is validated and what is not. The "Ported, not validated" entries -
IP networking over CYW43439, the inherited peripheral drivers
under concurrent cross-core use, and anything long-running - are where an
extra pair of hands would make the most difference. A result is worth having
whether it worked or not.

---

## How the port works

The RP2040 has two Cortex-M0+ cores and no cache coherency problem to solve,
but also no load-linked/store-conditional: atomicity comes from the hardware
spinlock block. This port builds a **recursive big kernel lock** on those
spinlocks and serializes all kernel state behind it, so both cores run the
same kernel image against one global ready queue.

Core 0 boots normally and is the sole SysTick timekeeper; core 1 is brought up
afterwards and explicitly disables its local SysTick, with the common handler
rejecting and counting any tick that arrives off core 0 anyway. Cross-core
work - waking a task resident on the other core, delivering a cyclic or alarm
callback to its assigned processor, remote task management - travels by
inter-processor interrupt, with reasons coalesced so a burst costs one IPI.

Peripheral IRQs are permanently owned by processor 1 (RP2040 core 0). The
inherited mSDI driver layer was converted from local interrupt masking to
SMP-aware serialization: an atomic `FastLock` counter, interrupt-preserving
cross-core locks around each task/IRQ handoff, and auto-cleared event flags in
place of sleep/wakeup pairs so a timeout/completion race cannot strand a stale
wakeup.

### What SMP support cost, in source terms

The SMP conversion changes **36 files** relative to the upstream Pico BSP
(`tron-forum/mtk3_bsp`, branch `pico_rp2040`, commit `15ed232`) â€” 11 added and
25 modified. That count excludes documentation, the qualification
applications, USB/Wi-Fi work, and unrelated Pico W baseline fixes.

**Added** â€” the SMP interfaces (`include/tk/smp.h`, `kernel/knlinc/smp.h`,
`kernel/knlinc/smp_lock.h`), the RP2040 backend
(`kernel/sysdepend/cpu/rp2040/smp_atomic.c`, `smp_rp2040.c`), and the generic
scheduler and locking (`kernel/tkernel/ready_queue_smp.c`, `smp_dispatch.c`,
`smp_lock.c`).

**Modified** â€” configuration to select `TK_SUPPORT_SMP` and `TK_MAX_CORE`; the
ARMv6-M context-switch, exception and dispatch paths to make saved task
context processor-aware; board init and idle handling to start core 1 and give
each core a private Thread-mode idle context; and the generic kernel
(`task.c`, `task_manage.c`, `task_sync.c`, `wait.c`, `timer.c`,
`time_calls.c`, `klock.c`, `cpuctl.c`) for affinity, remote task management,
cross-core wakeups and single-owner timekeeping.

The generic scheduler and locking policy is largely reusable. Atomics,
interrupts, secondary-core startup, context switching and the IPI transport
are target-specific â€” `kernel/sysdepend/cpu/rp2040/` is the principal
replacement point for another SoC.

Separately, the inherited peripheral drivers (`device/ser/`, `device/i2c/`,
`device/adc/`, `lib/libtk/fastlock.c`) were made SMP-safe. That work is not
required for the core SMP kernel to boot.

### Build dependencies (not redistributed here)

**The `arm-none-eabi` GCC toolchain** is required to build and is not included.

**The Raspberry Pi Pico SDK** is required only for the `CONSOLE=usb_cdc` and
`WIFI=cyw43` profiles, and is not included. Those profiles compile sources
from three of its submodules, each under its own licence and obtained with the
SDK rather than from here:

|---|---|---|

> [!IMPORTANT]
> The **cyw43-driver licence is not a plain BSD licence.** Raspberry Pi Ltd
> grants use "solely with the Licensor's microcontroller chip (RP2040) or any
> other semiconductor device produced by the Licensor. No other use is
> permitted." Its redistribution clause carries the same restriction. That
> covers this port, which is RP2040-only by definition, but it does mean the
> `WIFI=cyw43` profile cannot be carried to non-Raspberry-Pi silicon. The
> kernel, the qualified UART and USB-CDC profiles, and everything under
> `kernel/`, `include/`, `lib/libtk/` and `lib/libtm/` are unaffected.

**CYW43439 radio firmware** is distributed as a binary blob inside the Pico
SDK under Infineon's own terms. It is not redistributed here.

=== C:\Users\Yong Jun\school\INF2004-Embedded_Systems\project\INF2004_Project\testing\integration\hcsr04_host\test.c ===
#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdint.h>

#include "hcsr04.h"
#include "hcsr04_capture.h"

#define CHECK(condition) do { \
	if (!(condition)) { \
		printf("FAIL %s:%d: %s\n", __FILE__, __LINE__, #condition); \
		return 1; \
	} \
} while (0)

int main(void)
{
	uint16_t distance = 123U;
	uint32_t width = 77U;
	hcsr04_capture_t capture = {0};

	CHECK(hcsr04_distance_mm(1000U, 343000U, &distance));
	CHECK(distance == 172U);
	CHECK(hcsr04_distance_mm(5000U, 343000U, &distance));
	CHECK(distance == 858U);
	distance = 123U;
	CHECK(!hcsr04_distance_mm(0U, 343000U, &distance));
	CHECK(distance == 123U);
	CHECK(!hcsr04_distance_mm(1000U, 0U, &distance));
	CHECK(!hcsr04_distance_mm(UINT32_MAX, 400000U, &distance));
	CHECK(distance == 123U);
	puts("[TEST] OBS-U07 PASS");

	CHECK(!hcsr04_capture_init(&capture, 0U, 1000U));
	CHECK(hcsr04_capture_init(&capture, 30000U, 25000U));
	CHECK(!hcsr04_capture_init(&capture, 30000U, 25000U));
	CHECK(!hcsr04_capture_init(&(hcsr04_capture_t){0}, 30000U, 30001U));
	CHECK(hcsr04_capture_poll(&capture, false, 1U, &width) ==
	      HCSR04_CAPTURE_INVALID);
	CHECK(width == 77U);
	CHECK(hcsr04_capture_start(&capture, UINT32_MAX - 100U));
	CHECK(hcsr04_capture_poll(&capture, false, UINT32_MAX - 50U, &width) ==
	      HCSR04_CAPTURE_PENDING);
	CHECK(hcsr04_capture_poll(&capture, true, UINT32_MAX - 20U, &width) ==
	      HCSR04_CAPTURE_PENDING);
	CHECK(hcsr04_capture_poll(&capture, true, 29U, &width) ==
	      HCSR04_CAPTURE_PENDING);
	CHECK(hcsr04_capture_poll(&capture, false, 879U, &width) ==
	      HCSR04_CAPTURE_READY);
	CHECK(width == 900U);
	CHECK(hcsr04_capture_poll(&capture, false, 900U, &width) ==
	      HCSR04_CAPTURE_READY);
	CHECK(width == 900U);
	CHECK(hcsr04_capture_start(&capture, 1000U));
	CHECK(hcsr04_capture_poll(&capture, false, 31001U, &width) ==
	      HCSR04_CAPTURE_TIMEOUT);
	CHECK(capture.state == HCSR04_CAPTURE_FAULT);
	CHECK(width == 900U);
	CHECK(!hcsr04_capture_start(&capture, 32000U));
	CHECK(hcsr04_capture_reset(&capture));
	CHECK(hcsr04_capture_start(&capture, 32000U));
	CHECK(hcsr04_capture_reset(&capture));
	CHECK(capture.state == HCSR04_CAPTURE_IDLE);
	CHECK(hcsr04_capture_start(&capture, 33000U));
	CHECK(hcsr04_capture_reset(&capture));
	puts("[TEST] OBS-U08 PASS");
	return 0;
}

=== C:\Users\Yong Jun\school\INF2004-Embedded_Systems\project\INF2004_Project\testing\integration\i2c_host\test.c ===
#include <stdio.h>
#include <string.h>
#include <tk/tkernel.h>

#define __DEV_I2C_H__
#define __DEVINC_DEVDEF_H__
#define DEV_IIC_ENABLE 1
#define DEVCNF_I2C0_INTPRI 2
#define DEVCNF_I2C1_INTPRI 2
#define DEVCNF_I2C0_TMO 1000
#define DEVCNF_I2C1_TMO 1000
#define DEVCNF_I2C_RESET 0
#define DEVCNF_I2C_SETPINFUNC 0
#include "../../../device/i2c/sysdepend/rp2040/i2c_sysdep.h"

typedef struct { UW unit; } T_I2C_DCB;
typedef struct {
	UW sadr;
	INT snd_size;
	UB *snd_data;
	INT rcv_size;
	UB *rcv_data;
} T_I2C_EXEC;
enum { LLD_I2C_OPEN, LLD_I2C_CLOSE, LLD_I2C_READ, LLD_I2C_WRITE, LLD_I2C_EXEC };
#include "../../../device/i2c/sysdepend/rp2040/i2c_rp2040.c"

static UW mask, raw, commands[32], rx_value;
static unsigned command_count, received_count, stop_waits, stop_cleared;
static BOOL flag, suppress_stop, abort_first_command, early_disable;

UW in_w(UW address)
{
	if (address == I2C_INTR_STAT(1)) return (raw | I2C_INT_TX_EMPTY) & mask;
	if (address == I2C_DATA_CMD(1)) {
		raw &= ~I2C_INT_RX_FULL;
		return rx_value;
	}
	if (address == I2C_CLR_INTR(1)) { raw = 0; return 0; }
	if (address == I2C_CLR_STOP_DET(1)) {
		raw &= ~I2C_INT_STOP_DET;
		stop_cleared++;
		return 0;
	}
	if (address == I2C_CLR_TX_ABRT(1)) { raw &= ~I2C_INT_TX_ABRT; return 0; }
	return 0;
}

void out_w(UW address, UW value)
{
	if (address == I2C_INTR_MASK(1)) mask = value;
	if (address == I2C_ENABLE(1) && value == 0 && !stop_cleared &&
	    !suppress_stop && !abort_first_command) early_disable = TRUE;
	if (address != I2C_DATA_CMD(1)) return;
	if (command_count < sizeof(commands) / sizeof(commands[0]))
		commands[command_count++] = value;
	if (abort_first_command) {
		raw |= I2C_INT_TX_ABRT;
		return;
	}
	if (value & I2C_DATA_CMD_CMD_READ) {
		rx_value = 0x40U + received_count++;
		raw |= I2C_INT_RX_FULL;
	}
	if ((value & I2C_DATA_CMD_STOP) && !suppress_stop) raw |= I2C_INT_STOP_DET;
}

void set_w(UW address, UW value)
{
	if (address == I2C_INTR_MASK(1)) mask |= value;
}

void clr_w(UW address, UW value)
{
	if (address == I2C_INTR_MASK(1)) mask &= ~value;
}

void ClearInt(UINT intno) { (void)intno; }
void EnableInt(UINT intno, PRI priority) { (void)intno; (void)priority; }
ID tk_cre_flg(const T_CFLG *config) { (void)config; return 1; }
ER tk_del_flg(ID id) { (void)id; return E_OK; }
ER tk_set_flg(ID id, UINT bits) { (void)id; (void)bits; flag = TRUE; return E_OK; }
ER tk_clr_flg(ID id, UINT bits) { (void)id; (void)bits; flag = FALSE; return E_OK; }
ER tk_def_int(UINT intno, const T_DINT *config)
{
	(void)intno;
	(void)config;
	return E_OK;
}

ER tk_wai_flg(ID id, UINT bits, UINT mode, UINT *pattern, TMO timeout)
{
	unsigned count;
	(void)id;
	(void)bits;
	(void)mode;
	(void)timeout;
	if (ll_devcb[1].state == I2C_STS_WAIT_STOP) stop_waits++;
	for (count = 0; count < 64U && !flag; count++) {
		if (!in_w(I2C_INTR_STAT(1))) return E_TMOUT;
		i2c_inthdr(INTNO_I2C1);
	}
	if (!flag) return E_TMOUT;
	flag = FALSE;
	*pattern = 1U;
	return E_OK;
}

static void reset_mock(void)
{
	mask = raw = command_count = received_count = stop_waits = stop_cleared = 0U;
	flag = suppress_stop = abort_first_command = early_disable = FALSE;
	ll_devcb[1] = (T_I2C_LLDCB){.done_flgid = 1};
}

static BOOL transaction_test(void)
{
	UB send[2] = {0x28U, 0x57U};
	UB receive[6] = {0};
	T_I2C_EXEC exec = {0x19U, 1, send, 6, receive};
	BOOL ok;
	unsigned i;

	reset_mock();
	ok = dev_i2c_llctl(1, LLD_I2C_WRITE, 0x19U, 2U, (UW *)send) == 2 &&
	     command_count == 2U && commands[0] == 0x28U &&
	     commands[1] == (0x57U | I2C_DATA_CMD_STOP) &&
	     stop_waits == 1U && stop_cleared == 1U && !early_disable;
	reset_mock();
	ok = dev_i2c_llctl(1, LLD_I2C_READ, 0x19U, 1U, (UW *)receive) == 1 &&
	     command_count == 1U &&
	     commands[0] == (I2C_DATA_CMD_CMD_READ | I2C_DATA_CMD_STOP) &&
	     receive[0] == 0x40U && stop_waits == 1U && stop_cleared == 1U &&
	     !early_disable && ok;
	reset_mock();
	ok = dev_i2c_llctl(1, LLD_I2C_READ, 0x19U, 2U, (UW *)receive) == 2 &&
	     command_count == 2U && commands[0] == I2C_DATA_CMD_CMD_READ &&
	     commands[1] == (I2C_DATA_CMD_CMD_READ | I2C_DATA_CMD_STOP) &&
	     stop_waits == 1U && stop_cleared == 1U && !early_disable && ok;
	reset_mock();
	exec.rcv_size = 1;
	ok = dev_i2c_llctl(1, LLD_I2C_EXEC, 0, 0, (UW *)&exec) == 2 &&
	     command_count == 2U && commands[0] == 0x28U &&
	     commands[1] == (I2C_DATA_CMD_CMD_READ | I2C_DATA_CMD_RESTART |
			     I2C_DATA_CMD_STOP) &&
	     stop_waits == 1U && stop_cleared == 1U && !early_disable && ok;
	reset_mock();
	exec.rcv_size = 6;
	ok = dev_i2c_llctl(1, LLD_I2C_EXEC, 0, 0, (UW *)&exec) == 7 &&
	     command_count == 7U && commands[0] == 0x28U &&
	     commands[1] == (I2C_DATA_CMD_CMD_READ | I2C_DATA_CMD_RESTART) &&
	     commands[6] == (I2C_DATA_CMD_CMD_READ | I2C_DATA_CMD_STOP) &&
	     stop_waits == 1U && stop_cleared == 1U && !early_disable && ok;
	for (i = 2U; i < 6U; i++) ok = commands[i] == I2C_DATA_CMD_CMD_READ && ok;
	for (i = 0U; i < 6U; i++) ok = receive[i] == 0x40U + i && ok;
	return ok;
}

static BOOL failure_test(void)
{
	UB data[2] = {0x20U, 0x57U};
	BOOL ok;

	reset_mock();
	suppress_stop = TRUE;
	ok = dev_i2c_llctl(1, LLD_I2C_WRITE, 0x19U, 1U, (UW *)data) == E_TMOUT &&
	     stop_waits == 1U && mask == 0U;
	reset_mock();
	abort_first_command = TRUE;
	ok = dev_i2c_llctl(1, LLD_I2C_WRITE, 0x19U, 1U, (UW *)data) == E_IO &&
	     stop_waits == 1U && mask == 0U && ok;
	reset_mock();
	abort_first_command = TRUE;
	ok = dev_i2c_llctl(1, LLD_I2C_WRITE, 0x19U, 2U, (UW *)data) == E_IO &&
	     stop_waits == 0U && mask == 0U && ok;
	return ok;
}

int main(void)
{
	BOOL transactions = transaction_test();
	BOOL failures = failure_test();
	printf("[TEST] I2C-U01 %s  command sequencing and STOP completion\n",
	       transactions ? "PASS" : "FAIL");
	printf("[TEST] I2C-U02 %s  timeout and abort propagation\n",
	       failures ? "PASS" : "FAIL");
	return transactions && failures ? 0 : 1;
}

#ifndef IMU_I2C_HOST_TKERNEL_H
#define IMU_I2C_HOST_TKERNEL_H

#include <stddef.h>
#include <stdint.h>
#include <stdbool.h>

typedef int32_t ER;
typedef int32_t ID;
typedef int32_t W;
typedef uint32_t UW;
typedef int32_t SZ;
typedef uint8_t UB;
typedef uint32_t RELTIM;

typedef struct {
	UW hi;
	UW lo;
} SYSTIM;

#define IMPORT extern
#define EXPORT extern
#define E_OK       0
#define E_PAR     -17
#define E_OBJ     -41
#define E_IO      -57
#define E_TMOUT   -50
#define TD_READ    0x0001U
#define TD_WRITE   0x0002U
#define TD_UPDATE  0x0003U
#define TD_EXCL    0x0100U

IMPORT ID tk_opn_dev(const UB *devnm, uint32_t omode);
IMPORT ER tk_cls_dev(ID dd, uint32_t option);
IMPORT ER tk_swri_dev(ID dd, W start, const void *buf, SZ size, SZ *asize);

IMPORT ER tk_dly_tsk(RELTIM dlytim);

#endif
=== C:\Users\Yong Jun\school\INF2004-Embedded_Systems\project\INF2004_Project\testing\integration\imu_i2c_host\test.c ===
#include <stdio.h>
#include <string.h>

#include "lsm303dlhc_i2c.h"
#include "dev_i2c.h"

#define CHECK(condition) do { \
	if (!(condition)) { \
		printf("FAIL %s:%d: %s\n", __FILE__, __LINE__, #condition); \
		return 0; \
	} \
} while (0)

static uint8_t registers[256];
static UW current_time;
static unsigned close_count;
static unsigned open_count;
static unsigned config_writes;
static bool short_exec;
static imu_task_config_t captured_task_config;

ER imu_task_start(const imu_task_config_t *task_config)
{
	ER er;

	if (task_config == NULL) return E_PAR;
	captured_task_config = *task_config;
	if (captured_task_config.initialize != NULL) {
		er = captured_task_config.initialize(captured_task_config.read_context);
		if (er != E_OK) return er;
	}
	return E_OK;
}

ID tk_opn_dev(const UB *devnm, uint32_t omode)
{
	if (strcmp((const char *)devnm, "iicb") != 0 ||
	    omode != (TD_UPDATE | TD_EXCL))
		return E_PAR;
	open_count++;
	return 7;
}

ER tk_cls_dev(ID dd, uint32_t option)
{
	if (dd != 7 || option != 0U) return E_PAR;
	close_count++;
	return E_OK;
}

ER tk_swri_dev(ID dd, W start, const void *buf, SZ size, SZ *asize)
{
	if (dd != 7 || buf == NULL || asize == NULL) return E_PAR;
	if (start == TDN_I2C_EXEC) {
		T_I2C_EXEC *exec = (T_I2C_EXEC *)buf;
		uint8_t reg;
		SZ i;
		if (size != (SZ)sizeof(*exec) || exec->sadr > 0x7FU ||
		    exec->snd_size != 1 || exec->snd_data == NULL ||
		    exec->rcv_data == NULL)
			return E_PAR;
		reg = exec->snd_data[0];
		if ((reg & 0x7FU) == 0x28U && exec->rcv_size == 6) {
			for (i = 0; i < 6; i++)
				exec->rcv_data[i] = registers[(reg & 0x7FU) + i];
			registers[0x27] = 0U;
		} else if (exec->rcv_size == 1) {
			exec->rcv_data[0] = registers[reg & 0x7FU];
		} else {
			return E_PAR;
		}
		*asize = short_exec ? (SZ)sizeof(*exec) - 1 : (SZ)sizeof(*exec);
		return E_OK;
	}
	if (size != 2 || (start != 0x18 && start != 0x19)) return E_PAR;
	{
		const uint8_t *data = buf;
		registers[data[0]] = data[1];
		if (data[0] == 0x20U || data[0] == 0x23U) config_writes++;
	}
	*asize = size;
	return E_OK;
}

ER tk_get_otm(SYSTIM *pk_tim)
{
	if (pk_tim == NULL) return E_PAR;
	pk_tim->hi = 0U;
	pk_tim->lo = current_time;
	return E_OK;
}

ER tk_dly_tsk(RELTIM dlytim)
{
	current_time += dlytim;
	return E_OK;
}

static void reset_fake(void)
{
	memset(registers, 0, sizeof(registers));
	current_time = 100U;
	close_count = 0U;
	open_count = 0U;
	config_writes = 0U;
	short_exec = false;
	registers[0x0F] = 0x33U;
	registers[0x27] = 0x08U;
	registers[0x28] = 0x00U;
	registers[0x29] = 0x10U;
	registers[0x2A] = 0x00U;
	registers[0x2B] = 0x00U;
	registers[0x2C] = 0x80U;
	registers[0x2D] = 0x3EU;
}

static lsm303dlhc_i2c_config_t config(void)
{
	lsm303dlhc_i2c_config_t result = {0};
	(void)lsm303dlhc_i2c_default_config(&result, 2U);
	return result;
}

static int test_fresh_sample(void)
{
	lsm303dlhc_i2c_t sensor = {0};
	lsm303dlhc_i2c_config_t setup = config();
	accel_data_t sample = {0};
	uint32_t timestamp = 0U;

	reset_fake();
	CHECK(lsm303dlhc_i2c_init(&sensor, &setup) == E_OK);
	CHECK(open_count == 1U && sensor.initialized && !sensor.faulted);
	CHECK(setup.address == 0x19U && setup.mapping.sign[0] == -1 &&
	      setup.mapping.sign[1] == 1 && setup.mapping.sign[2] == -1);
	CHECK(registers[0x20] == 0x57U && registers[0x23] == 0x00U);
	CHECK(config_writes == 3U);
	CHECK(lsm303dlhc_i2c_read(&sensor, &sample, &timestamp) == E_OK);
	CHECK(sample.raw_x == -256 && sample.raw_y == 0 && sample.raw_z == -1000);
	CHECK(sample.ax < -2.63f && sample.ax > -2.65f &&
	      sample.az < -10.29f && sample.az > -10.31f);
	CHECK(sample.i2c_ok && timestamp == 100U);
	CHECK(lsm303dlhc_i2c_close(&sensor) == E_OK);
	CHECK(close_count == 1U && registers[0x20] == 0U);
	return 1;
}

static int test_identity_mismatch(void)
{
	lsm303dlhc_i2c_t sensor = {0};
	lsm303dlhc_i2c_config_t setup = config();

	reset_fake();
	registers[0x0F] = 0U;
	CHECK(lsm303dlhc_i2c_init(&sensor, &setup) == E_IO);
	CHECK(close_count == 1U && !sensor.opened && sensor.faulted);
	CHECK(config_writes == 0U);
	return 1;
}

static int test_short_transfer(void)
{
	lsm303dlhc_i2c_t sensor = {0};
	lsm303dlhc_i2c_config_t setup = config();

	reset_fake();
	short_exec = true;
	CHECK(lsm303dlhc_i2c_init(&sensor, &setup) == E_IO);
	CHECK(close_count == 1U && !sensor.opened);
	return 1;
}

static int test_not_ready_latches_without_output_change(void)
{
	lsm303dlhc_i2c_t sensor = {0};
	lsm303dlhc_i2c_config_t setup = config();
	accel_data_t sample = {.raw_x = 12, .raw_y = 34, .raw_z = 56};
	uint32_t timestamp = 77U;

	reset_fake();
	CHECK(lsm303dlhc_i2c_init(&sensor, &setup) == E_OK);
	registers[0x27] = 0U;
	CHECK(lsm303dlhc_i2c_read(&sensor, &sample, &timestamp) == E_TMOUT);
	CHECK(sample.raw_x == 12 && sample.raw_y == 34 && sample.raw_z == 56);
	CHECK(timestamp == 77U && sensor.faulted);
	CHECK(lsm303dlhc_i2c_read(&sensor, &sample, &timestamp) == E_OBJ);
	CHECK(lsm303dlhc_i2c_close(&sensor) == E_OK);
	return 1;
}

static int test_task_start_wires_transport(void)
{
	lsm303dlhc_i2c_config_t setup = config();
	imu_task_config_t task_config = {
		.period_ms = 10U,
		.sample_max_age_ms = 30U,
		.odometry_max_age_ms = 50U
	};
	accel_data_t sample = {0};
	uint32_t timestamp = 0U;

	reset_fake();
	CHECK(lsm303dlhc_i2c_start_imu_task(&setup, &task_config) == E_OK);
	CHECK(captured_task_config.period_ms == 10U);
	CHECK(captured_task_config.sample_max_age_ms == 30U);
	CHECK(captured_task_config.odometry_max_age_ms == 50U);
	CHECK(captured_task_config.initialize != NULL);
	CHECK(captured_task_config.read == lsm303dlhc_i2c_read);
	CHECK(captured_task_config.read_context != NULL);
	CHECK(captured_task_config.read(captured_task_config.read_context,
				       &sample, &timestamp) == E_OK);
	CHECK(sample.raw_x == -256 && sample.raw_z == -1000 && timestamp == 100U);
	CHECK(lsm303dlhc_i2c_close(captured_task_config.read_context) == E_OK);
	return 1;
}

int main(void)
{
	lsm303dlhc_i2c_config_t defaults = {0};
	CHECK(lsm303dlhc_i2c_default_config(&defaults, 10U) == E_OK);
	CHECK(defaults.address == 0x19U && defaults.data_ready_timeout_ms == 10U);
	CHECK(defaults.mapping.axis[0] == 0U && defaults.mapping.axis[1] == 1U &&
	      defaults.mapping.axis[2] == 2U);
	CHECK(defaults.mapping.sign[0] == -1 && defaults.mapping.sign[1] == 1 &&
	      defaults.mapping.sign[2] == -1);
	CHECK(lsm303dlhc_i2c_default_config(&defaults, 0U) == E_PAR);
	if (!test_fresh_sample() || !test_identity_mismatch() ||
	    !test_short_transfer() || !test_not_ready_latches_without_output_change() ||
	    !test_task_start_wires_transport())
		return 1;
	puts("[TEST] IMU-I2C-U01 PASS");
	puts("[TEST] IMU-I2C-U02 PASS");
	puts("[TEST] IMU-I2C-U03 PASS");
	puts("[TEST] IMU-I2C-U04 PASS");
	return 0;
}

# Integration â€” test log

Tests that two or more subsystems work together. There is one test (or more) for every interface listed in TEAM.md section 5. Both owners agree on the test and sign off the result.

See `testing/README.md` for how to run tests and record results.

## Test cases

Define a test here before running it. Test IDs never change.

| ID | What is tested | Requirement / interface | Method | Steps / how | Expected result |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|

# Testing

All test records for the project live in this folder. A test only counts as
done when it is **written down here with its result and a saved log**.

```
testing/
â”œâ”€â”€ README.md              this guide
â”œâ”€â”€ subsystem1/            one folder per subsystem, kept by its owner
â”‚   â”œâ”€â”€ TEST_LOG.md        test cases + results
â”‚   â””â”€â”€ logs/              saved console output, photos, scope captures
â”œâ”€â”€ ...
â”œâ”€â”€ subsystem5/
â”œâ”€â”€ integration/           tests across two or more subsystems (shared)
â””â”€â”€ system/                tests of the whole product against its requirements (shared)
```

## Three levels of test

| Level | ID format | Tests what | Written by | Where |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Unit | `S1-U01`, `S2-U01`, â€¦ | one subsystem on its own | the subsystem owner | `subsystemN/TEST_LOG.md` |
| Integration | `INT-01`, â€¦ | an interface between subsystems (TEAM.md section 5) | the owners on both sides | `integration/TEST_LOG.md` |
| System | `SYS-01`, â€¦ | the finished product against a requirement | whole team | `system/TEST_LOG.md` |

Test IDs never change and are never reused, even if a test is dropped.

## How to run and record a test

The RP2040 I2C register-mock tests run on a host, not on the robot. From
`testing/integration/i2c_host` in Ubuntu/WSL:

```sh
gcc -std=c11 -Wall -Wextra -Werror -Wno-old-style-declaration -I. test.c -o /tmp/i2c_host_test
/tmp/i2c_host_test
```

They do not verify electrical timing or actual I2C hardware.

The LSM303DLHC transport tests also run against a host I2C/API mock, not the
robot. From `testing/integration/imu_i2c_host`:

```sh
gcc -std=c11 -Wall -Wextra -Werror -Isys -I../../../app_program/imu \
  test.c ../../../app_program/imu/lsm303dlhc_i2c.c \
  ../../../app_program/imu/lsm303dlhc_reader.c \
  ../../../app_program/imu/lsm303dlhc_accel.c -o imu_i2c_host_test
./imu_i2c_host_test
```

These tests verify software configuration and mocked transfer handling only;
they do not verify the sensor, address selection, bus timing, or timestamp
accuracy on the robot.

The HSR04 distance conversion test is hardware-independent. From
`testing/integration/hcsr04_host`:

```sh
gcc -std=c11 -Wall -Wextra -Werror -I../../../app_program/obstacle \
  test.c ../../../app_program/obstacle/hcsr04.c \
  ../../../app_program/obstacle/hcsr04_capture.c -o hcsr04_host_test
./hcsr04_host_test
```

It checks only Echo pulse-width conversion and input validation, not GPIO,
level shifting, sensor acquisition, or servo operation.

1. **Define it first.** Add a row to the *Test cases* table in the right
   `TEST_LOG.md`: ID, what it checks, how, and the expected result.
2. **Automate it if you can.** Add a `TEST_CHECK` with the same ID to
   `app_program/subsystemN/subsystemN_test.c` (see `app_program/test_report.h`).
   A test that needs eyes or instruments (an LED blinks, a motor turns, a
   scope shows 1 kHz) is a *manual* test. Write its steps in the table instead.
3. **Build the test image and flash it.**
   ```sh
   cd build_make
   make TEST=1 -j8        # software startup tests; template examples disabled
   make TEST=1 EXAMPLES=1 -j8  # also run template tests; drives GP19
   ```
   With `EXAMPLES=1`, the test image runs every template subsystem's tests
   at power-up and prints one
   `[TEST] <ID> PASS|FAIL` line per check. (A normal `make` leaves the tests out.)
4. **Save the console output** to `logs/`, named `YYYY-MM-DD_<what>.txt`:
   ```sh
   # macOS (find the port name with: ls /dev/cu.usbmodem*)
   cat /dev/cu.usbmodem1101 | tee ../testing/subsystem1/logs/2026-10-14_unit.txt
   # Linux / WSL with usbipd
   cat /dev/ttyACM0 | tee ../testing/subsystem1/logs/2026-10-14_unit.txt
   ```
   Start the command first, then reset the Pico. Press Ctrl-C when it is done.
   On Windows with PuTTY, use *Session â†’ Logging â†’ All session output*.
   For manual tests, save photos or scope screenshots in `logs/` the same way
   (keep each file under a few MB). Videos go in `videos/` instead (see below).
5. **Record the result.** Add a row to the *Results* table: date, test ID,
   the commit you tested (`git rev-parse --short HEAD`), who ran it,
   PASS/FAIL, and the log file. Commit the log and the table together.

## Videos of tests and demos

A manual test (an LED, a motor, a sensor reacting) is best shown with a short
video. Record 15â€“60 s showing the test ID and the hardware, share it as an
**unlisted YouTube** link (the only way videos are shared), list it in
**`videos/README.md`**, and put the link in the *Log / evidence* column of
the test's result row. `videos/README.md` explains how.

## Rules

- **Never edit or delete an old result.** If a test fails, record the FAIL,
  fix the code, re-run, and add a new PASS row. The history is the evidence.
- **Always record the commit.** A result is meaningless without the exact
  code it was run on. Commit your work *before* testing.
- **A FAIL needs a note** saying what went wrong and, later, which commit fixed it.
- Before merging a change into `main`, re-run your subsystem's unit tests.
  Before a demo or hand-in, run every test and record the full set.
=== C:\Users\Yong Jun\school\INF2004-Embedded_Systems\project\INF2004_Project\tools\elf2uf2\boot\uf2.h ===
/*
 * Copyright (c) 2020 Raspberry Pi (Trading) Ltd.
 *
 * SPDX-License-Identifier: BSD-3-Clause
 */

#ifndef _BOOT_UF2_H
#define _BOOT_UF2_H

#include <stdint.h>
#include <assert.h>

/** \file uf2.h
*  \defgroup boot_uf2_headers boot_uf2_headers
*
* \brief Header file for the UF2 format supported by a RP2xxx chip in BOOTSEL mode
*/

#define UF2_MAGIC_START0 0x0A324655u
#define UF2_MAGIC_START1 0x9E5D5157u
#define UF2_MAGIC_END    0x0AB16F30u

#define UF2_FLAG_NOT_MAIN_FLASH          0x00000001u
#define UF2_FLAG_FILE_CONTAINER          0x00001000u
#define UF2_FLAG_FAMILY_ID_PRESENT       0x00002000u
#define UF2_FLAG_MD5_PRESENT             0x00004000u
#define UF2_FLAG_EXTENSION_FLAGS_PRESENT 0x00008000u

// Extra family IDs
#define CYW43_FIRMWARE_FAMILY_ID    0xe48bff55u

// Bootrom supported family IDs
#define RP2040_FAMILY_ID            0xe48bff56u
#define ABSOLUTE_FAMILY_ID          0xe48bff57u
#define DATA_FAMILY_ID              0xe48bff58u
#define RP2350_ARM_S_FAMILY_ID      0xe48bff59u
#define RP2350_RISCV_FAMILY_ID      0xe48bff5au
#define RP2350_ARM_NS_FAMILY_ID     0xe48bff5bu
#define BOOTROM_FAMILY_ID_MIN       RP2040_FAMILY_ID
#define BOOTROM_FAMILY_ID_MAX       RP2350_ARM_NS_FAMILY_ID

// Defined for backwards compatibility
#define FAMILY_ID_MAX               BOOTROM_FAMILY_ID_MAX

// 04 e3 57 99
#define UF2_EXTENSION_RP2_IGNORE_BLOCK 0x9957e304

struct uf2_block {
    // 32 byte header
    uint32_t magic_start0;
    uint32_t magic_start1;
    uint32_t flags;
    uint32_t target_addr;
    uint32_t payload_size;
    uint32_t block_no;
    uint32_t num_blocks;
    uint32_t file_size; // or familyID;
    uint8_t  data[476];
    uint32_t magic_end;
};

static_assert(sizeof(struct uf2_block) == 512, "uf2_block not sector sized");

#endif
=== C:\Users\Yong Jun\school\INF2004-Embedded_Systems\project\INF2004_Project\tools\elf2uf2\elf.h ===
/*
 * Copyright (c) 2020 Raspberry Pi (Trading) Ltd.
 *
 * SPDX-License-Identifier: BSD-3-Clause
 */

#ifndef _ELF_H
#define _ELF_H

#include <stdint.h>

#define ELF_MAGIC 0x464c457fu

#define EM_ARM 0x28u

#define EF_ARM_ABI_FLOAT_HARD 0x00000400u

#define PT_LOAD 0x00000001u

#pragma pack(push, 1)
struct elf_header {
    uint32_t    magic;
    uint8_t     arch_class;
    uint8_t     endianness;
    uint8_t     version;
    uint8_t     abi;
    uint8_t     abi_version;
    uint8_t     _pad[7];
    uint16_t    type;
    uint16_t    machine;
    uint32_t    version2;
};

struct elf32_header {
    struct elf_header common;
    uint32_t    entry;
    uint32_t    ph_offset;
    uint32_t    sh_offset;
    uint32_t    flags;
    uint16_t    eh_size;
    uint16_t    ph_entry_size;
    uint16_t    ph_num;
    uint16_t    sh_entry_size;
    uint16_t    sh_num;
    uint16_t    sh_str_index;
};

struct elf32_ph_entry {
    uint32_t type;
    uint32_t offset;
    uint32_t vaddr;
    uint32_t paddr;
    uint32_t filez;
    uint32_t memsz;
    uint32_t flags;
    uint32_t align;
};
#pragma pack(pop)

#endif

# Videos

Short videos of tests and demos running on the real hardware. A video shows
what a log cannot: the LED blinking, the motor turning, the robot reaching
the line. List **every** video in the table at the bottom of this page.

## What to record

- **Short:** 15â€“60 seconds. One test or one feature per video.
- **Say what it is.** Show or say the test ID (e.g. `S2-U03`, `INT-01`) or
  the demo name at the start.
- **Show the commit.** Before recording, run `git rev-parse --short HEAD` and
  keep the terminal in shot for a moment, or put it in the table below.
- **Show the result.** Keep both the hardware and, if it matters, the
  console output in view.
- **Landscape**, steady, good light. No music needed.

## How to share it: unlisted YouTube only

1. Upload the video to YouTube with visibility set to **Unlisted**: anyone
   with the link can watch it, but it does not show up in search. Not
   *Private* (only invited accounts can watch, so the link won't work for
   your team or instructor), and not *Public*.
2. Copy the video's link (e.g. `https://youtu.be/...`).

**Do not commit video files to this repo or attach them on GitHub.** A video
in the repo stays in the git history for good, even if deleted later, and
slows every clone for the whole team. Video files are ignored by
`.gitignore` for this reason.

Then add a row to the table below. If the video is evidence for a test, also
put its link in the *Log / evidence* column of that test's row in
`testing/.../TEST_LOG.md`.

# Hardware Verification & Integration Guide

## Pin Configuration Summary

This guide helps you verify that all sensors and motors are wired correctly and communicating.

### I2C Bus (Sensors: IMU, possibly other I2C devices)

| Pin | Function | Device | Notes |
|-----|----------|--------|-------|
| GPIO4 | I2C0_SDA | LSM303DLHC Accel/Mag | Pull-up required (~4.7kÎ© to 3.3V) |
| GPIO5 | I2C0_SCL | LSM303DLHC Accel/Mag | Pull-up required (~4.7kÎ© to 3.3V) |
| 3.3V | Power | I2C devices | 50-100mA available for all I2C peripherals |
| GND | Ground | All | Common ground with robot power system |

**Verify I2C Communication:**

```bash
cd testing/integration/imu_i2c_host
gcc -std=c11 -Wall -Wextra -Werror -Isys -I../../../app_program/imu \
  test.c ../../../app_program/imu/lsm303dlhc_i2c.c \
  ../../../app_program/imu/lsm303dlhc_reader.c \
  ../../../app_program/imu/lsm303dlhc_accel.c -o imu_test
./imu_test
```

If this passes on your dev machine, I2C driver code is correct. If on-robot test fails:
- Check SDA/SCL voltages: should be ~3.3V when idle, drop to 0V during transfers
- Measure pull-up resistors: should be ~4.7kÎ© to 3.3V
- Verify LSM303DLHC address with `i2cdetect` tool (address 0x19 for accel, 0x1E for mag)

---

### ADC Channels (Line Sensors)

| Pin | ADC Channel | Function | Notes |
|-----|-------------|----------|-------|
| GPIO26 | ADC0 | Line sensor 0 (typically left) | 0-3.3V analog input |
| GPIO27 | ADC1 | Line sensor 1 (center-left) | 0-3.3V analog input |
| GPIO28 | ADC2 | Line sensor 2 (center-right) | 0-3.3V analog input |
| GPIO29 | ADC3 | Line sensor 3 (right) | 0-3.3V analog input |
| (+more) | ADC0-3 | Additional sensors | Max 4 ADC inputs on RP2040 |

**Expected Voltage Ranges:**
- White surface: 0.0V - 0.5V (dark)
- Black surface: 2.5V - 3.3V (bright due to IR reflection)
- Gray (line boundary): 1.0V - 2.0V

**Verify ADC Reading:**

1. Open console, monitor `line_adc_reader_test()` output
2. Point sensor at white surface: should read low values
3. Point sensor at black line: should read high values
4. Sweep sensor across line: values should transition smoothly

---

### GPIO: Encoders

| Pin | Function | Motor | Notes |
|-----|----------|-------|-------|
| GPIOXX | Encoder A | Left wheel | Rising edge = count increment |
| GPIOXX | Encoder B | Left wheel | Determines direction (forward/reverse) |
| GPIOXX | Encoder A | Right wheel | Rising edge = count increment |
| GPIOXX | Encoder B | Right wheel | Determines direction |

**Check in:** `app_program/odometry/encoder.h` for actual GPIO assignments

**Verify Encoder Signals:**

1. Spin left wheel by hand while watching console
2. Should see left encoder count increase
3. Spin right wheel: right count should increase
4. Reverse both wheels: counts should increment regardless of direction

If counts don't change:
- Check GPIO pins connected to encoder outputs
- Verify encoder outputs are logic-level (0V/3.3V or 0V/5V with level shifter)
- Use oscilloscope to confirm edges on GPIO pins

---

### GPIO: Motor PWM

| Pin | Function | Motor | Notes |
|-----|----------|-------|-------|
| GPIOXX | PWM A | Left motor | 0-100% duty cycle controls speed |
| GPIOXX | PWM B | Right motor | 0-100% duty cycle controls speed |

**Check in:** `app_program/motion/motor_driver.h` for actual GPIO assignments

**Verify Motor PWM:**

1. Robot on blocks (wheels off ground)
2. Send VELOCITY command for forward motion
3. Listen for motor sound/vibration
4. Check PWM with oscilloscope:
   - Frequency: typically 1-20 kHz
   - Duty cycle: 0% (stopped) to 100% (full speed)
   - When moving forward: both motors at same duty cycle

If motors don't move:
- Check motor driver power supply voltage
- Verify PWM pins connected to motor driver control inputs
- Check motor power connectors (may need separate 6-12V supply)

---

### GPIO: Ultrasonic Sensors (HC-SR04)

| Pin | Function | Direction | Notes |
|-----|----------|-----------|-------|
| GPIOXX | Trigger | Output (Pico â†’ Sensor) | 10Î¼s pulse starts measurement |
| GPIOXX | Echo | Input (Sensor â†’ Pico) | High time = distance (us/58 = cm) |
| (repeat) | Trigger | Front | Multiple sensors possible |
| (repeat) | Echo | Front | |

**Check in:** `app_program/obstacle/obs_pico_port.h` for actual GPIO assignments

**Verify Ultrasonic Sensor:**

```bash
cd testing/integration/hcsr04_host
gcc -std=c11 -Wall -Wextra -Werror -I../../../app_program/obstacle \
  test.c ../../../app_program/obstacle/hcsr04.c \
  ../../../app_program/obstacle/hcsr04_capture.c -o hcsr04_test
./hcsr04_test
```

On robot:
1. Place hand 20cm from sensor
2. Check console for distance_mm â‰ˆ 200
3. Move hand away, distance should increase
4. Wait 30ms between measurements (sensor needs cool-down)

If distance is always 0xFFF (max):
- Check trigger pin connected and toggling
- Check echo pin connected and has pull-up resistor
- Verify sensor power (5V, 15-50mA), not 3.3V
- Use oscilloscope to measure echo pulse width

---

### GPIO: Other Hardware

| Pin | Function | Device | Typical Usage |
|-----|----------|--------|---------------|
| GPIOXX | Status LED | On-board or external | Blink pattern shows system state |
| GPIOXX | Safety button | Emergency stop | Momentary connection to GND |

**Check in:** Subsystem header files for exact GPIO assignments

---

## Testing Protocol: Hardware Integration Checklist

### Step 1: Power & Connection Verification

- [ ] Pico W powered via USB (5V, 0.5A minimum for base; 1A recommended with sensors)
- [ ] All sensor power lines connected (3.3V for I2C/logic, 5V for ultrasonic)
- [ ] Common ground between Pico and all peripherals
- [ ] No shorts between power and ground
- [ ] Multimeter: Measure 3.3V and 5V rails under load

**Test Command:**
```bash
make TEST=1 -j8
# Look for startup message confirming microcontroller is alive
```

---

### Step 2: I2C Bus Verification

**On Robot:**
1. Build and flash `make TEST=1`
2. Watch console for I2C error messages
3. If test hangs on I2C init, check:
   - Voltage on SDA/SCL pins (should be ~3.3V idle)
   - Pull-up resistors present and correct value
   - No short circuits on I2C pins
   - I2C address correct (default 0x19 for accel, 0x1E for mag)

**On Host (Linux/WSL):**
```bash
cd testing/integration/imu_i2c_host
gcc -std=c11 -Wall -Wextra -Werror -Isys -I../../../app_program/imu \
  test.c ../../../app_program/imu/lsm303dlhc_i2c.c \
  ../../../app_program/imu/lsm303dlhc_reader.c \
  ../../../app_program/imu/lsm303dlhc_accel.c -o imu_test
./imu_test
# If passes on host, I2C protocol code is correct
# If fails on robot, issue is electrical/I2C bus
```

---

### Step 3: ADC (Line Sensor) Verification

1. **White surface test:**
   - Point sensor over white paper
   - Console should show low ADC values (< 500)

2. **Black surface test:**
   - Point sensor over black tape
   - Console should show high ADC values (> 2000)

3. **Threshold test:**
   - Gradually transition from white to black
   - Confidence value should be 255 when clearly over line
   - Lateral error should be 0 when centered

**If ADC reads all zeros:**
- Check GPIO pin connected to ADC channel
- Verify capacitive coupling on ADC input (if present)
- Check ADC reference voltage (should be 3.3V)

**If ADC reads all 4095:**
- Check sensor is powered
- Verify sensor output voltage (0-3.3V, not 0-5V without level shifter)
- Check for stuck connection (floating pin)

---

### Step 4: GPIO Interrupt Verification (Encoders & Ultrasonic)

**Encoder Test:**
1. Spin left wheel by hand
2. Console should show increasing left encoder count
3. Spin right wheel: right count increases
4. Check direction bits are correct (forward vs. backward)

**If counts don't increase:**
- Verify GPIO pins in oscilloscope (should see 0V/3.3V edges)
- Check encoder polarity (may need to swap A/B channels)
- Verify interrupt handler is registered in GPIO dispatcher

**Ultrasonic Test:**
1. Place hand in front of sensor
2. Console should show distance decreasing as hand approaches
3. Remove hand: distance returns to 3000+ mm (no object)

**If ultrasonic always reads max (0xFFF):**
- Check echo capture is enabled in `gpio_irq_router`
- Verify echo pin connected and toggling on oscilloscope
- Check trigger pulse is 10Î¼s width (may be too short/long)

---

### Step 5: Motor & Speed Control Verification

**Visual/Audio Test:**
1. Command VELOCITY mode (linear_velocity = 50 mm/s)
2. Listen for motor humming
3. Feel wheel resistance (should rotate with small effort)

**Odometry Feedback Test:**
1. Command forward motion
2. Measure wheel movement distance with tape
3. Compare against reported odometry distance
4. Target: Within 5% accuracy

**PWM Signal Test (Oscilloscope):**
1. Connect scope to PWM pin
2. Command motion
3. Observe:
   - Frequency: 1-20 kHz (typical)
   - Duty cycle changes with speed command
   - Both motors same frequency/phase

**If motors don't move:**
- Check motor power supply voltage (6-12V, separate from Pico 5V)
- Verify motor driver IC is powered
- Check direction pins (if using H-bridge)
- Measure back-EMF when spinning by hand (indicates coils OK)

---

### Step 6: WiFi & Telemetry Verification

**Prerequisites:**
- WiFi SSID and password correct in `config/wifi_secrets.h`
- MQTT broker IP and port correct
- Robot in range of WiFi network

**Test Steps:**
1. Open console
2. Look for WiFi connection message (usually within 5 seconds of boot)
3. Verify MQTT broker connection
4. Use MQTT client to subscribe to telemetry topic:
   ```bash
   mosquitto_sub -h 192.168.1.10 -t "mtk3smp-pico/heartbeat" -v
   ```
5. Should see heartbeat message every 10 seconds

**If WiFi fails to connect:**
- Verify SSID is correct (case-sensitive)
- Check password in `wifi_secrets.h`
- Confirm WiFi network is 2.4GHz (Pico W doesn't support 5GHz)
- Check antenna connection on Pico W (small metal piece should be at angle)

**If MQTT connection fails:**
- Verify broker IP address correct
- Ping broker from Pico machine: `ping 192.168.1.10`
- Check firewall allowing MQTT port 1883
- Verify broker is running (`sudo systemctl status mosquitto`)

---

## Debugging Tools & Techniques

### Console Output Analysis

```
[TEST] ODO PASS       âœ“ Odometry tests passed
[TEST] IMU FAIL       âœ— IMU test failed - check I2C
[TEST] OBS PASS       âœ“ Obstacle detection OK
ERROR: [code]         âœ— System error - halt here
```

**Common errors:**
- `ERROR: I2C timeout` - SDA/SCL stuck low, check pull-ups
- `ERROR: ADC not ready` - ADC not initialized, check config
- `ERROR: MQTT connection failed` - Network/broker issue
- `ERROR: Motor stalled` - Odometry doesn't match motor PWM

### Oscilloscope / Logic Analyzer

**Key signals to check:**
1. **I2C bus:** SDA and SCL should toggle together, not stuck
2. **Encoder A/B:** Rising edges should match wheel rotation
3. **Ultrasonic echo:** Pulse width proportional to distance
4. **Motor PWM:** Frequency stable, duty cycle changes with speed command
5. **GPIO interrupts:** Signal edges aligned with interrupt handler timing

### Multimeter Checks

| Measurement | Expected | If Wrong |
|---|---|---|
| 3.3V rail (idle) | 3.3V Â± 0.1V | Voltage regulator failure or short |
| 5V rail (idle) | 5.0V Â± 0.2V | Power supply issue |
| I2C SDA (idle) | 2.8-3.3V | Stuck low: short or missing pull-up |
| Motor supply | 6-12V | Motor power issue |
| GND continuity | < 0.1Î© | Broken ground connection |

### Adding Debug Output

Edit `app_program/subsystemN/subsystemN.c` to add debug prints:

```c
if (result < E_OK) {
    tm_printf((UB*)"[DEBUG] I2C read failed: %d at address 0x%02x\n", 
              (int)result, i2c_address);
}
```

Rebuild: `make TEST=1 -j8`

   - Time < 30 seconds

2. **Obstacle test:** Line track with box 50cm away
   - Robot approaches, detects, stops
   - Resumes after obstacle removed

3. **Long run:** 30 minutes continuous operation
   - No crashes, consistent performance
   - Memory not leaking (log file size stable)

## Sensor Datasheets & Reference

### LSM303DLHC (Accelerometer/Magnetometer)

- **I2C Address:** 0x19 (accelerometer), 0x1E (magnetometer)
- **Full scale:** Â±2g, Â±4g, Â±8g, Â±16g (configurable)
- **Sensitivity:** 1mg/LSB (at Â±2g)
- **Datasheet:** Search "LSM303DLHC datasheet" for detailed register map

### HC-SR04 (Ultrasonic Range)

- **Measurement range:** 2cm - 400cm
- **Accuracy:** Â±3%
- **Trigger pulse:** 10Î¼s at least once per 60ms
- **Echo formula:** Distance (cm) = Echo time (Î¼s) / 58
- **Datasheet:** Search "HC-SR04 datasheet" for timing diagrams

### Typical Line Sensor

- **Type:** Reflective IR sensor array (8-16 channels)
- **Output:** Analog voltage 0-3.3V per channel
- **Threshold:** ~1.5-2V for line/no-line decision
- **Typical values:** White: 0.2V, Black: 2.8V

# Quick Test Checklist - Get Started in 15 Minutes

## Phase 1: Verify Build & Unit Tests (5 mins)

```bash
cd build_make
make TEST=1 -j8
```

Expected: Build completes successfully, creates `mtk3pico_smp1_usb_cdc_wifi.uf2`

---

## Phase 2: Flash & Capture Output (5 mins)

1. **Prepare Pico for flash:**
   - Hold BOOTSEL button on Pico W
   - Plug in USB cable
   - Release BOOTSEL (Pico appears as mass storage device labeled RPI-RP2)

2. **Copy firmware:**
   ```bash
   # macOS/Linux
   cp build_make/mtk3pico_smp1_usb_cdc_wifi.uf2 /Volumes/RPI-RP2/
   # Or drag-and-drop in file explorer
   ```

3. **Open console in new terminal:**
   ```bash
   # macOS
   cat /dev/cu.usbmodem* | tee test_output.txt
   
   # Linux / WSL
   cat /dev/ttyACM0 | tee test_output.txt
   ```

4. **Power on Pico:** Unplug and re-plug USB (or press RESET button)

5. **Watch for tests:** You should see:
   ```
   === micro T-Kernel on Raspberry Pi Pico W ...
   [TEST] INT-03 PASS  vehicle buffers
   [TEST] ODO PASS     odometry calculations
   [TEST] ODO-PRODUCER PASS
   [TEST] LINE PASS    line classification
   ...
   [TEST] === test run end ===
   ```

6. **Save output:** Stop with Ctrl-C, log is saved to `test_output.txt`

---

## Phase 3: Quick Subsystem Health Checks (5 mins)

After unit tests pass, manually verify each subsystem:

### âœ“ Encoders Working?
- **Check:** Console should show encoder pulse counts incrementing
- **If fails:** Verify encoder GPIO pins connected, check for voltage on pins

### âœ“ Line Sensors Ready?
- **Check:** Hover line sensor over black and white surfaces
- **Expected:** Readings should change significantly
- **If fails:** Check ADC channel configuration, verify sensor power

### âœ“ IMU Responding?
- **Check:** Tilt robot 45 degrees, check console for roll/pitch values
- **Expected:** `roll_mdeg` should show ~45000
- **If fails:** Check I2C address (0x19 or 0x1E), verify pull-ups on SDA/SCL

### âœ“ Ultrasonic Working?
- **Check:** Place hand 20cm from HC-SR04, watch console
- **Expected:** distance_mm â‰ˆ 200
- **If fails:** Check trigger pin (GPIO), echo pin (GPIO), verify 5V power

### âœ“ Motors Spinning?
- **Check:** Listen for motor hum when motion task starts
- **Expected:** Gentle humming or rotation
- **If fails:** Check motor driver power, verify PWM pins connected

### âœ“ WiFi Connected?
- **Check:** Console shows MQTT connection message
- **Expected:** "MQTT connected to 192.168.1.10:1883"
- **If fails:** Verify SSID and password in `config/wifi_secrets.h`, restart

## Common Issues & Quick Fixes

| Issue | Fix |
|-------|-----|
| "command not found: arm-none-eabi-gcc" | Add to ~/.bashrc: `export PATH="$HOME/tools/arm-gnu-toolchain-*/bin:$PATH"` |
| "PICO_SDK_PATH not found" | Set: `export PICO_SDK_PATH="$HOME/pico/pico-sdk"` |
| No USB device appears | Try different USB cable, different USB port, hold BOOTSEL longer |
| Tests hang after boot | Check power supply (needs 5V, 1A minimum), restart Pico |
| Console garbage text | Check baud rate is 115200, try different terminal app |
| MQTT tests fail | Verify broker IP in `config/wifi_secrets.h`, check WiFi SSID/password |
