# micro T-Kernel 3.0 project for Raspberry Pi Pico W

A starting point for your project: micro T-Kernel 3.0 running on **both cores**
of the Pico W's RP2040, with a USB serial console and the WiFi driver built in.

You need a **Raspberry Pi Pico W**, a USB cable, and a computer running
macOS, Linux, or Windows with **WSL (Ubuntu)**. On Windows, run every
command below inside the WSL Ubuntu terminal.

---

## 1. Set up the Pico C SDK and the compiler

You need two separate things:

| What | Why | Where it comes from |
|---|---|---|
| **Pico C SDK** | USB and WiFi driver source code | `git clone` (step 1.2) |
| **Arm GNU Toolchain** (`arm-none-eabi-gcc`) | the compiler | a separate download (step 1.3) |

> âš ï¸ **The SDK does NOT include the compiler.** You must install the compiler
> on its own and put its `bin` folder on your `PATH` (step 1.4).

**1.1 Install the basic tools: git, make and a host C++ compiler**

```sh
# macOS
xcode-select --install

# Linux / WSL (Ubuntu)
sudo apt update && sudo apt install -y git make g++
```

**1.2 Download the Pico C SDK** (any folder works; this guide uses `~/pico`)

```sh
mkdir -p ~/pico && cd ~/pico
git clone --branch 2.2.0 https://github.com/raspberrypi/pico-sdk.git
cd pico-sdk
git submodule update --init lib/tinyusb lib/cyw43-driver
```

The `submodule` line is required: it downloads the USB and WiFi drivers.
(SDK 2.3.0 has also been tested and works.)

**1.3 Install the Arm GNU Toolchain (the compiler)**

- **macOS / Windows (WSL) / Linux:** download from
  <https://developer.arm.com/downloads/-/arm-gnu-toolchain-downloads>.
  Pick the **AArch32 bare-metal target (arm-none-eabi)** package for your
  computer. For WSL, choose the **Linux x86_64** `.tar.xz`.
  Extract it, e.g. to `~/tools/arm-gnu-toolchain-<version>/`.
- **Linux / WSL shortcut:** `sudo apt install -y gcc-arm-none-eabi`
  installs it straight onto your PATH. If you use this, skip the PATH line in 1.4.

Find the folder that contains `arm-none-eabi-gcc`. It is the toolchain's
**`bin`** folder, e.g. `~/tools/arm-gnu-toolchain-15.3.rel1/bin`.

**1.4 Tell your shell where the compiler and SDK are**

Add these two lines to the end of `~/.zshrc` (macOS) or `~/.bashrc` (Linux/WSL),
**changing both paths to match your computer**:

```sh
export PATH="$HOME/tools/arm-gnu-toolchain-15.3.rel1/bin:$PATH"   # folder that contains arm-none-eabi-gcc
export PICO_SDK_PATH="$HOME/pico/pico-sdk"                         # the SDK from step 1.2
```

Open a **new** terminal, then check both:

```sh
arm-none-eabi-gcc --version     # must print a version, not "command not found"
ls $PICO_SDK_PATH               # must list the SDK files
```

If `arm-none-eabi-gcc` is "not found", your `PATH` does not point to the
toolchain's `bin` folder. Fix that before going further.

---

## 2. Clone this repo and build the `.uf2` file

**2.1 Clone your project**

```sh
cd ~/pico
git clone <URL-of-this-repo> my-project
cd my-project
```

**2.2 Build**

```sh
cd build_make
make -j8
```

A successful build ends with a line like
`tools/elf2uf2 mtk3pico_smp1_usb_cdc_wifi.elf mtk3pico_smp1_usb_cdc_wifi.uf2`.
Your firmware is **`build_make/mtk3pico_smp1_usb_cdc_wifi.uf2`**.

If `make` stops with an error, it tells you which one is wrong:
`arm-none-eabi-gcc not found` means fix `PATH`, and
`PICO_SDK_PATH is not set` means fix `PICO_SDK_PATH` (step 1.4).

To build a single-core, UART-only or test version, see section 3.

**2.3 Flash it onto the Pico W**

1. Hold the **BOOTSEL** button and plug the Pico W into USB, then release.
   A drive called **RPI-RP2** appears.
2. Copy the `.uf2` file onto that drive. On WSL, run `explorer.exe .` in
   `build_make` to open the folder in Windows. The Pico reboots by itself.

**2.4 See the output**

Open the Pico's USB serial port at 115200 baud:

```sh
screen /dev/tty.usbmodem* 115200      # macOS   (quit: Ctrl-A then K)
screen /dev/ttyACM0 115200            # Linux
```

On Windows, use PuTTY or the VS Code *Serial Monitor* on the new COM port.
Template example tasks are disabled by default while the robot is migrated.
With `EXAMPLES=1`, the template reports appear and subsystem 1 drives GP19
for an LED. Disconnect the robot's GP19 encoder wire before using that build.

**2.5 Write your own code**

Your code lives in **`app_program/`**. Every `.c` file there, and in its
subfolders, is compiled automatically.

- **`app_main.c` â†’ `usermain()` is where the program starts.** It creates
  vehicle message buffers first. With `EXAMPLES=1`, it then calls every
  example subsystem's `subsystemN_init()` and `subsystemN_start()`.
- Do not copy the source `integration/car_main.c` as another entry point.
  Its default path is a bare-metal `main()` that launches core-specific loops;
  ÂµT-Kernel already owns startup and calls `usermain()`. The source file's
  optional `PICOCAR_USE_UTKERNEL` branch delegates to the source
  `vehicle_system_run()`, not this target's startup configuration. Map its
  responsibilities as follows:
  - `vc_core()` / `vc_task()` â†’ the vehicle-controller and motion tasks;
    kernel tasks and message buffers replace the hand-launched core loop.
  - `obs_core()` / `obs_step()` â†’ the obstacle task, which owns its sensor and
    scan operations.
  - `line_status_init()` / `car_measurements_init()` â†’ line/barcode and
    odometry producers, which publish copied messages instead of shared state.
  - Source `line_steering.c` is preserved under `app_program/controller/` as a
    hardware-independent decision module. It retains its PWM intent values
    but is not wired to the motion task or started by application startup.
  - `car_link_init()` â†’ still needs a target-local input provider. Do not call
    blocking `tm_getchar()` from the controller callback; the callback must
    return within its task period.
  Vehicle tasks remain opt-in until local-input binding, motion authorization,
  and hardware configurations are verified.
- **One folder per student:** `app_program/subsystem1/` â€¦ `subsystem5/`.
  Subsystems 1â€“4 hold a working example, one task each (LED, producer,
  consumer, monitor). Together they use the main micro T-Kernel APIs and
  pass data between subsystems. Subsystem 5 is an empty template.
  Replace the examples with your own subsystems.
- `subsystemN.h` is that subsystem's public interface: the object IDs and
  data formats the other subsystems may use.
- `app.h` holds the definitions shared by the whole team.
- **Drivers go in your subsystem folder too**, as a `drv_<name>.c` /
  `drv_<name>.h` pair, e.g. `subsystem2/drv_ultrasonic.c`. They are compiled
  automatically. Do not add drivers under `device/`: that folder holds the
  kernel's own serial, I2C and ADC drivers, and adding to it means editing
  kernel and makefile files shared by the whole team.

**Working as a team:** record owners, pins, priorities and the interfaces
between subsystems in **`TEAM.md`**, and record every test in **`testing/`**
(see `testing/README.md`). `make TEST=1` tests the vehicle buffers, the
hardware-independent encoder/odometry calculations, line classification, and
Code 39 barcode decoding, IMU processing, obstacle profiling, wheel control
controller safety predicates and GPIO interrupt routing;
`make TEST=1 EXAMPLES=1` also runs the template subsystem tests. Short videos
of tests and demos are shared as unlisted YouTube links, listed in **`videos/`**.

The line/barcode task consumes line odometry and publishes line observations
and decoded barcodes. It and the ADC reader are not started by default.
The reader exclusively opens the kernel ADC, configures only the explicitly
selected GP26â€“GP28 inputs, and requires per-sensor thresholds and polarity.
Its `line_adc_reader_default_config()` imports the original repository's
barcode/center/right ADC channels (2/1/0), 1000-count thresholds, and
black-above polarity; callers still choose the acquisition-span limit.
The supplied pin map assigns left to GP28 (ADC2), center to GP27
(ADC1), and right to GP26 (ADC0); the GPIO numbers were confirmed despite
reversed ADC0/ADC2 labels in the supplied table. The sensor used for barcode
acquisition is not yet confirmed, and no thresholds are assumed.
Verify wiring, pin ownership and 3.3 V-compatible sensor outputs before use;
GP29 is reserved for the radio. A conversion or timing failure latches the
reader faulted and retains ADC ownership until restart. ADC pins remain analog
after a normal close. The barcode timestamp precedes its eight conversions;
the configured maximum span rejects completed reads that took too long, but
does not bound blocking inside the ADC driver or detect missed edges. The
driver can wait indefinitely for ADC readiness or synchronous completion;
independent safety supervision is required. Keep the reader storage alive
until a normal close, or until reboot after a fault, because an interrupted
device request may still access its conversion buffer.
Startup requires an owned sensor-reader callback, barcode settings,
sampling period and maximum sample/odometry age. The reader timestamps
barcode acquisition using the same operating clock as odometry. It stops on stale, missing or
out-of-sequence odometry, unsynchronized barcode edges, read or delivery errors,
and retries fault delivery. Consumers must also fail safe on stale data when a
fault cannot be delivered. The scanner uses the mean of polarity-normalized
wheel count changes, so compatible wheel count scales are required. Tests use
simulated samples; sensor thresholds, encoder direction, sampling/odometry
timing and barcode behavior must be verified on hardware before use.
`line_adc_reader_start_task()` optionally binds the actual ADC reader to the
line/barcode task; ADC open and pin setup occur inside that task. It requires
caller-supplied channel selection, thresholds, polarity and timing limits.
No application startup calls it, and barcode acquisition on GP28 is not
confirmed by the pin plan.

The IMU processor consumes simulated or caller-provided acceleration,
distance and timestamps; it does not start a task or access the sensor.
Its thresholds, mounting orientation and odometry inputs need hardware
verification before use in the vehicle. The single-owner IMU producer converts
successful accelerometer samples and fresh, ordered odometry into copied
estimate messages. Only accelerometer-derived roll and pitch are marked
valid; dynamic acceleration can make them inaccurate. Yaw and gyro fields are
not measured. The message includes packed terrain telemetry and latched shock and
tilt indicators. The caller must report rejected samples, check sample age,
retain future odometry until its timestamp, and deliver messages; no IMU task
or hardware reader is started by default. The inactive IMU task owns its
reader callback, consumes the dedicated odometry buffer, and publishes
estimates. It stops on invalid or stale inputs, clock, scheduling or delivery
errors, and retries fault delivery. It requires explicitly configured sampling
and age limits. Consumers must also fail safe on stale estimates if fault
delivery fails or the reader blocks; real timing and sensor behavior are
unverified.

The reported IMU is an LSM303DLHC on I2C1 GP2/GP3. The portable accelerometer
decoder expects a verified fresh six-byte read configured for little-endian,
normal-mode +/-2g operation, and an explicit vehicle-axis mapping. It does not
configure the sensor or access I2C. Bus ownership and any other devices are
not yet confirmed. The kernel I2C driver is disabled; if explicitly enabled,
startup initializes only I2C1 on GP2/GP3 as `iicb`. Confirm ownership before
enabling it. I2C0 is still configured on motor pins GP8/GP9 and must not be
initialized until its pins are reassigned.
The RP2040 I2C driver's transaction sequencing has host-level register-mock
tests, but it has not been validated on the physical bus. Timeouts and bus
faults must be handled independently; do not treat a software build as sensor
verification.
The inactive LSM303DLHC reader adapter connects a supplied fresh-transfer
callback to the IMU task. The transport must configure the verified mode,
establish that each sample is new, supply all six bytes and a valid acquisition
timestamp, and own the bus; the adapter cannot independently verify those
claims. An opt-in I2C1 transport is now available in
`app_program/imu/lsm303dlhc_i2c.c`: it opens `iicb` exclusively, accepts the
sensor address as configuration (the imported default is 0x19), checks
WHO_AM_I and control registers, configures 100 Hz normal-mode +/-2g acquisition,
uses the original 1.05 mg/LSB scale and X/Y/Z signs (-,+,-), waits for
ZYXDA, and reads all six output bytes. It supplies timestamps on the
`tk_get_otm` clock that precede the corresponding acquisition. The ready wait
is capped at 100 ms; kernel I2C transaction timeouts can add up to the driver's
configured timeout for each bus operation. Any transport error latches a
fault. `lsm303dlhc_i2c_start_imu_task()` wires this reader into the IMU task
with static context lifetime; sensor initialization and reads execute in the
IMU task context. No application startup calls it.
I2C remains disabled by default; bus sharing and timestamps have not been
verified on physical hardware. The original source also initialized the
magnetometer, but the current vehicle message/processor path consumes only
accelerometer data.

The obstacle task has an opt-in Pico callback adapter in
`app_program/obstacle/obs_pico_port.c`. It uses the shared GPIO dispatcher for
Echo edges and runs the task on core 0. It is not registered or started by
application startup. Its default configuration deliberately leaves Echo
voltage safety and servo endpoint confirmations false and servo pulse limits
unset; do not enable it until the Echo signal is confirmed 3.3 V-safe and the
servo endpoints are measured. The original source's Pico-car port itself says
it was not compiled or hardware-tested, and its servo values are placeholders.

The obstacle profile module processes simulated distance scans only. Its
geometry and thresholds are placeholders; a clear scan or computed bypass is
not proof that the path is safe. The obstacle producer emits typed ahead and
scan observations with an explicitly configured emergency distance; emergency
indications are not latched. Scan distance is the minimum measured range,
while target distance belongs to the selected profile. Consumers must check
freshness, latch safety conditions and independently veto motion. Bypass
directions are advisory and unverified. The inactive obstacle task monitors
ahead and starts scans only on controller request. With the reported single
scanning sensor, forward monitoring pauses during scans: the controller must
confirm the vehicle is stopped before requesting a scan and keep it stopped
until a fresh ahead observation arrives after scanning or cancellation.
The task cannot independently stop motors or verify that precondition. Its
optional initialization callback, polling callback and stop callback share the
obstacle task's owner context. The polling and stop callbacks must return
promptly; a blocked callback or failed fault delivery requires independent
freshness supervision. The reported ultrasonic sensor is an HSR04, but its
Echo voltage/level shifting and the servo's safe pulse limits are unknown.
Do not connect a 5 V Echo directly to the Pico W or activate the sensor/servo
until those details are verified. The opt-in callback adapter described above
is not started by application startup and has not been hardware-tested.
The inactive `hcsr04_distance_mm()` helper converts a measured Echo high-pulse
width to millimetres using an explicitly supplied sound speed; it does not
measure the Echo pulse or initialize GPIO. The companion Echo capture state
machine validates rising/falling edge timestamps, timer wrap and timeout, but
also does not access GPIO; a hardware integration must capture both edges
without missing the short pulse.

Wheel-speed control calculates PWM magnitudes only. The opt-in hardware motion
task can now apply them through `app_program/motion/motor_driver.c`; application
startup does not call it. The driver starts braked with emergency stop latched
and only its initializing task may control it. Hardware mode requires an
explicit, fresh ARM command while stopped, followed by a fresh VELOCITY command;
STOP does not re-arm it. It applies the original left/right motor polarity
mapping, but wiring and direction must be checked with the wheels raised.
Keep I2C0 disabled or move its GP8/GP9 pins before using the motors. Gains,
encoder signs, emergency-stop behavior, independent stopping and real-time
behavior remain unverified; this port has only build-level validation.
The motion feedback adapter calculates signed wheel speeds from consecutive,
fresh odometry messages with explicit per-wheel calibration and timing limits.
The standalone motion control step combines it with the PID for straight
forward or reverse movement. It waits for two fresh samples, rejects unexpected
wheel direction, and latches a zero-output fault on stale or invalid feedback.
It must be checked periodically even without new messages; it cannot stop
hardware or replace an independent watchdog if its caller stalls. Neither
component consumes a buffer or drives motors. The feedback age and interval
limits must not exceed 250 ms.

The motion task has a simulation entry point and a separate opt-in hardware
entry point. Both consume the dedicated command and odometry buffers; only
hardware mode publishes motion status, and neither entry point is started by
default. Straight velocity commands use signed cm/s and expire from their
timestamp; turns and nonzero angular velocities are rejected. Hardware mode
requires the controller to send ARM only after its safety checks; an unarmed
velocity command faults. Ordinary stop takes precedence over movement already
queued, while emergency stop latches a fault until restart. Start the controller
before the hardware motion task so the status buffer has a consumer; status
delivery failure faults and stops the motion task. The controller must handle
faults, supervise freshness independently, and have active consumers for the
other odometry buffers. Its task period must be no more than 50 ms to keep up
with the 100 ms status publication interval. The hardware path has only
build-level validation and has not been flashed or tested on the robot.

The motion-status message reports task-commanded signed PWM, command activity
and latched faults, with sequence numbers and timestamps. It does not report
ARM acceptance, is not motor telemetry, and cannot prove that the vehicle has
physically stopped. The controller requires a fresh status reporting zero
commanded output before requesting ARM, and faults on stale, invalid, faulted
or non-idle status. Do not enable VELOCITY control until an explicit arm-ready
acknowledgement and an independently validated physical-stop contract exist.

The standalone controller safety monitor validates line, IMU and obstacle
freshness and ordering, latches faults, and requires a new go edge after a
veto. Its thresholds require robot validation. During a scan it blocks
following until a post-scan ahead reading; the caller must independently verify
the vehicle is stopped before requesting the scan and keep it stopped.
The monitor does not start a controller task, send commands, verify a motor
stop, or clear an emergency stop. The controller and motion task must enforce
those responsibilities when integrated, including a watchdog independent of
the monitor's periodic checks.
An opt-in controller monitor task connects these checks to the line, IMU,
forward-obstacle and fault buffers for diagnostics only. Start it before the
producers and never alongside another consumer of those buffers. It never
arms motion, requests scans, sends commands or verifies a physical stop; keep
motor power safely disabled. It steps the advisory state machine with go held
low; faults enter the faulted state and stop monitoring until restart. This is
not the vehicle controller task or a substitute for independent motor safety.

The standalone controller state machine is an advisory simulation of waiting,
following, blocked, scanning and faulted states. Its single owner supplies
sensor observations to its safety monitor and calls it every cycle with a
current trusted local go input. A go signal already high at startup, or held
high through a veto, cannot arm or rearm; release and press it again after
conditions clear. Faults and emergency stop remain latched until restart.
The state machine does not start a task, steer, send motion commands, request
scans or verify physical stopping. `FOLLOWING` is permission to follow, not
proof that a vehicle is following a line. Scanning still requires independently
verified stopping and a fresh post-scan ahead observation. Remote commands are
not connected. Independent motor-stop and watchdog mechanisms remain required.

An opt-in `vehicle_controller_task` now connects this safety gate to the line,
IMU, obstacle, fault and motion-status buffers and sends
ARM/STOP/emergency-stop messages. Its required local-input callback supplies
trusted GO and emergency-stop levels; dashboard commands alone cannot arm. It
sends no VELOCITY commands yet because the motion task still rejects steering
commands. The motion status is commanded state, not physical feedback. The
controller exclusively consumes those buffers, so do not start it alongside
`controller_monitor_start()`. It is not started by application startup and has
not been hardware-tested.

The opt-in application WiFi service supports simulated callbacks or an outbound
MQTT bridge backend.
The template's built-in `cyw43_utk_task` owns CYW43, lwIP and MQTT; neither the
application task nor its callbacks may call those APIs. The service consumes copied
telemetry and reports connection status; it holds a message when the backend
reports temporary backpressure and faults on stale or invalid telemetry and
delivery failures. A ready controller must consume status and faults, and
publishers must handle full buffers. No service backend or remote command
handling is started by default. Callbacks must return
promptly and an independent supervisor must detect a stalled service.
The standalone command decoder understands the earlier dashboard's two-byte
packet, but is not connected to the service or controller. Its sequence wraps
at 255, and the packet has no sender timestamp or authentication; decoding
alone cannot prove freshness, prevent replay or authorize movement.

The outbound MQTT bridge adds a separate copied message buffer for the network
owner. After buffer initialization and startup tests, a single startup owner may
call `wifi_service_start_mqtt(period_ms, status_period_ms, telemetry_max_age_ms,
owner_max_age_ms)` in a `NET=lwip MQTT=1` build. The last argument bounds the
age of network-owner link snapshots and must be at least the service period;
allow for scheduling and the network poll interval. A missing, stale or
inconsistent snapshot stops the service. Until the first owner poll, status is
disconnected for at most that interval. Retry and error counts are not measured
by this backend and are reported as zero. `wifi_telemetry_make()` builds a
copied telemetry message containing the legacy five-byte packet. Its seven-bit
wire sequence comes from the low bits of the message's 32-bit sequence; the
producer must advance that sequence only after successful queueing and use the
operating-clock timestamp of its oldest input. The opt-in single-owner
`wifi_telemetry_producer_init()` and `wifi_telemetry_producer_send()` manage
that sequence, check input age and return queue-full errors without advancing
it. Start the service first, use a producer age limit no greater than the
service's, and handle all send errors. A clock fault latches until restart.
The producer does not acquire or verify sensor data. The legacy state map must be agreed
with the controller. It has no unknown battery value, so do not fabricate one
or publish until a verified battery measurement is available. The network owner
publishes the payload on `robot/telemetry` at QoS 0. No source of verified
telemetry data is connected, and neither bridge nor vehicle tasks start by default. Submissions
report a full queue; acceptance is not broker delivery, and QoS 0 can lose data.
The bridge retains a pending packet on temporary MQTT backpressure and stops
until reboot on stale/invalid data, clock or publish errors, reporting a
communication fault when possible. Stopping the service requests bridge shutdown;
one publish already in progress may still complete, and queued packets are
abandoned. Restart requires reboot, including if service task startup fails
after bridge creation. A stalled network owner or full fault queue
requires independent supervision. Queued packets are checked for age on dequeue;
they can become stale while disconnected. MQTT in this template is unencrypted;
use only on a trusted network. No incoming commands are connected.

### Read-only dashboard

`dashboard/` is a host-side Node.js application, separate from the Pico
firmware. Run `npm ci` and `npm start` in that directory. It subscribes only to
`robot/telemetry`, decodes the target's five-byte packet, ignores retained
packets, and marks data stale after five seconds. Configure `MQTT_URL`,
`MQTT_USER`, `MQTT_PASSWORD`, and optionally `PORT` through the process
environment. The HTTP server binds to `127.0.0.1`; do not expose it directly to
an untrusted network. The dashboard has no command-publish or barcode-mapping
route and is not a safety monitor. Firmware telemetry remains opt-in and has no
verified sensor-data producer connected by default.

The encoder bring-up uses the shared GPIO dispatcher. All interrupt clients
must be registered by one core-0 startup owner before the dispatcher starts;
live registration is not supported. A robot test reported left -2500 and right
2500 counts after one forward wheel revolution, and a 20 cm circumference.
Baseline deltas, reverse direction, repeatability and IRQ timing remain to be
verified. Pending edge bits do not record edge order or timestamps; fast or
simultaneous transitions can be missed.
The encoder source must be registered before dispatcher startup while the wheels
are stationary. Snapshots require a task pinned to core 0. Ambiguous or
inconsistent edge observations latch the snapshot invalid until restart; other
missed edges may still go undetected. The bring-up diagnostic prints invalid
counts for troubleshooting only.

The odometry producer can build messages from caller-supplied encoder snapshots
and send copies to separate line and IMU buffers. Motion publication is opt-in
through `odometry_task_start_with_motion()` and uses its own buffer; start its
consumer before opting in or the full buffer will stop odometry. An odometry task connects the
source and producer but is not started by the default image. Before enabling it,
verify both wheel polarities and calibrations, initialize the buffers and start
the shared dispatcher with the wheels stationary. Its startup caller must be
pinned to core 0 and handle any error. The task latches faults and stops
publishing after invalid input, clock, scheduling or delivery failures; it
reports the fault to the controller buffer, retrying if delivery fails. Fault
timestamps use the last known operating time (zero if unavailable). Consumers
must reject stale data and detect sequence gaps, including if fault delivery is
blocked. Motion feedback derives wheel speeds from the copied counts; no
wheel-speed messages are provided.
The task and encoder hardware have not been tested on the robot.

Pins: use **GP0â€“GP22 and GP26â€“GP28**. GP0/GP1 are free unless your team
builds with `CRASH_REPORT=1` (section 3), which needs them. Never use
GP23/GP24/GP25/GP29: they are wired to the Pico W radio. The Pico W's
on-board LED is also on the radio, so use an external LED instead.

After editing, run `make -j8` again in `build_make` and copy the new `.uf2`.

---

## 3. Build options: `SMP`, `CONSOLE`, `WIFI`, `TEST`, `EXAMPLES`, `CRASH_REPORT`

`make` on its own uses the defaults. To build differently, put options
after `make`. They can be combined in any order:

```sh
make -j8                          # defaults: SMP=1 CONSOLE=usb_cdc WIFI=cyw43 TEST=0 EXAMPLES=0 CRASH_REPORT=0
make SMP=0 -j8                    # single-core build
make CONSOLE=uart WIFI=none -j8   # no USB console, radio off (no Pico SDK needed)
make TEST=1 -j8                   # vehicle buffer test, no example tasks
make TEST=1 ODOMETRY_TEST=1 -j8  # live encoder bring-up, no motors
make TEST=1 EXAMPLES=1 -j8        # also run template examples and their tests; GP19 is driven
make CRASH_REPORT=1 -j8           # also see crash reports, on GP0/GP1 (uses those pins)
```

To set several options at once, list them all, separated by spaces. For
example, to track down a crash in the test build while running on one core only:

```sh
make SMP=0 TEST=1 CRASH_REPORT=1 -j8
#  -> build_make/mtk3pico_smp0_usb_cdc_wifi_crash_test.uf2
```

Any option you leave out keeps its default (here `CONSOLE=usb_cdc` and
`WIFI=cyw43`). Options are not remembered: give them on every `make`, as
the next plain `make` goes back to the defaults.

| Option | Values (**default**) | What it does | Image name | C macro your code can test |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `SMP` | `0`, **`1`** | `1` runs the kernel on both RP2040 cores; `0` uses core 0 only | `smp1` / `smp0` | `TK_SUPPORT_SMP` (1 or 0) |
| `CONSOLE` | **`usb_cdc`**, `uart` | where `tm_printf` output goes. `usb_cdc` = USB cable; `uart` = UART0 only (GP0 TX / GP1 RX, needs a USB-serial adapter) | `_usb_cdc` / `_uart` | `TM_CONSOLE_USB_CDC` |
| `WIFI` | **`cyw43`**, `none` | `cyw43` powers up the Pico W radio and its driver task; `none` leaves the radio off | `_wifi` / *(nothing)* | `TM_WIFI_CYW43` |
| `TEST` | **`0`**, `1` | `1` runs the vehicle buffer test; template tests also require `EXAMPLES=1` | `_test` / *(nothing)* | `APP_TEST` (1 or 0) |
| `EXAMPLES` | **`0`**, `1` | `1` starts the template examples; subsystem 1 drives GP19 | `_examples` / *(nothing)* | `APP_EXAMPLES` (1 or 0) |
| `ODOMETRY_TEST` | **`0`**, `1` | `1` starts live encoder bring-up on GP16â€“19; requires `TEST=1` and `EXAMPLES=0` | `_odometry` / *(nothing)* | `APP_ODOMETRY_TEST` (1 or 0) |
| `CRASH_REPORT` | **`0`**, `1` | `1` also copies the console to UART0 on **GP0 (TX) / GP1 (RX)**, 115200 baud, so you can see the last messages and the kernel's crash report (see below). **Uses GP0/GP1.** `CONSOLE=uart` always does this. | `_crash` / *(nothing)* | `TM_CONSOLE_UART` (1 or 0) |

Encoder bring-up prints signed counts and individual wheel distances every
second. GP16/17 are left A/B and GP18/19 are right A/B. It assumes 2500
transitions per wheel revolution and a 200 mm circumference; verify the signs,
calibration and circumference on each wheel. It does not initialize motors,
send odometry messages or start the vehicle controller. Rotate wheels slowly
by hand first: GPIO edge interrupts can miss transitions at high speeds.

**Why `CRASH_REPORT` exists.** USB output is not sent the moment you call
`tm_printf`: it waits in a buffer until the kernel's USB task sends it. If the
program crashes, no task runs again, so the last messages and the kernel's
crash report never reach your USB terminal. The terminal just goes quiet.
UART output *is* sent at once, even inside the crash handler. So when you
need to know why the Pico crashed or froze, build with `CRASH_REPORT=1`,
connect a USB-serial adapter (adapter RX â†’ GP0, adapter GND â†’ GND), and read
it at 115200 baud. Normal `tm_printf` output on USB works the same with
either setting. If a subsystem has claimed GP0/GP1 in `TEAM.md`, unplug that
hardware before using `CRASH_REPORT=1`.

The options are part of the image name, e.g.
`mtk3pico_smp1_usb_cdc_wifi_test.uf2`, so you can always tell which build
you are flashing. Switching options needs no `make clean`: the makefile
notices and recompiles everything.

Using an option in C code:

```c
#if TK_SUPPORT_SMP
    ctsk.tskatr |= TA_ASSPRC;   /* core affinity only exists on the dual-core build */
#endif

#if APP_TEST
    /* code that only exists in the test build */
#endif
```

### Where the options live in the makefile

The options are handled in **`build_make/pico_rp2040.mk`**. To find each piece, search the file for the
names in the left column (e.g. `grep -n "TEST" build_make/pico_rp2040.mk`).
Each option goes through the same steps:

| Step | What to look for | Example for `TEST` |
|---|---|---|
| 1. Default value | `NAME ?= value` near the top of the file | `TEST ?= 0` |
| 2. Allowed values | an `$(error Unknown ...)` check just below it | `$(error Unknown TEST '$(TEST)'; use 0 or 1)` |
| 3. Image-name suffix | `NAME_SUFFIX :=` and the `EXE_FILE :=` line | `TEST_SUFFIX := _test` |
| 4. C macro for the code | a `CFLAGS += -D...` line | `CFLAGS += -DAPP_TEST=$(TEST)` |
| 5. Rebuild on change | the `PROFILE_ID :=` line | `...-test$(TEST)` |
| 6. Extra source files (`CONSOLE`, `WIFI` only) | an `ifeq ($(CONSOLE),usb_cdc)` or `ifeq ($(WIFI),cyw43)` block that adds the USB/radio files and SDK include paths | â€” |

- **Changing a default:** edit the `?=` line from step 1, e.g.
  `CONSOLE ?= uart`. Every `make` without that option then uses the new value.
- **Adding your own option** (say `DEBUG=1` to turn on extra prints): copy
  how `TEST` is done. Add steps 1 to 5 next to the `TEST` lines, then
  use `#if APP_DEBUG` in your code. **Step 5 matters:** without it, switching
  the option can quietly link files that were compiled with the old setting.
- **Kernel settings** such as the maximum number of tasks, semaphores or
  mailboxes are not make options. They are in `config/config.h` (`CNF_MAX_...`).

The makefile also has `NET=lwip` and `MQTT=1` (TCP/IP and MQTT over WiFi).
They need the SDK's `lib/lwip` submodule and a `config/wifi_secrets.h`
(copy `config/wifi_secrets.example.h`). This guide does not cover them.

---

T-License 2.2. This dual-core RP2040 port is derived from the official
Î¼T-Kernel 3.0 BSP (`tron-forum/mtk3_bsp`, branch `pico_rp2040`).*

# Team and subsystems

The tables are pre-filled with the **example** in `app_program/` (marked
*example*). Replace those rows with your own as you replace the example code.
The examples are disabled by default. `EXAMPLES=1` enables them and drives
GP19; disconnect the robot's GP19 encoder wire before using that build.

Fill this in at the start of the project and keep it up to date. It is the
single place that says **who owns what**, so that five people can work in
one firmware image without clashing over pins, priorities, or kernel objects.

A **subsystem** is one student's part of the product, with its own folder.
It may contain several tasks and own several peripherals (see section 3).

Rule: **only the owner edits a subsystem's folders.** To change another
subsystem, ask its owner or open a pull request for them to review.

## 1. Members and subsystems

| # | Subsystem (what it does) | Student ID | GitHub username | Code | Tests |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | *example:* LED heartbeat on GP19 | | | `app_program/subsystem1/` | `testing/subsystem1/` |
| 2 | *example:* Producer: makes a sample every 300 ms | | | `app_program/subsystem2/` | `testing/subsystem2/` |
| 3 | *example:* Consumer: collects samples into statistics | | | `app_program/subsystem3/` | `testing/subsystem3/` |
| 4 | *example:* Monitor: prints reports, pauses the LED | | | `app_program/subsystem4/` | `testing/subsystem4/` |
| 5 | | | | `app_program/subsystem5/` | `testing/subsystem5/` |

Shared files (`app_program/app_main.c`, `app_program/app.h`, this file)
are changed only by agreement of the team. The exception is claiming an empty
row in this file (a pin, a peripheral, an interface ID), which anyone may do
(section 2).

## 2. Pins

One owner per pin. Claim a pin here **before** you wire or code it.

**Why:** two subsystems using the same pin is one of the most common and
hardest-to-find mistakes in a team project. The code builds without any
error, but the hardware misbehaves. Each subsystem can pass its own tests
and then fail once they are combined, and two outputs driving the same pin
against each other can damage it. Writing the claim down first means a clash
is spotted when someone edits this table, not hours later on the bench.

**No instructor check needed.** The team keeps this table itself. You do not
need the instructor, or the rest of the team, to check or approve a claim:
if the row is empty, fill it in and commit. If two people claim the same
pin, they agree between themselves who moves. Only the *reserved* rows are
fixed.

| Pin | Owner | Used for |
|---|---|---|
| GP23, GP24, GP25, GP29 | *reserved* | Pico W radio |
| GP0 | | (taken whenever the team builds with `CRASH_REPORT=1`) |
| GP1 | | (taken whenever the team builds with `CRASH_REPORT=1`) |
| GP2 | IMU | I2C1 SDA |
| GP3 | IMU | I2C1 SCL |
| GP4 | obstacle detection | ultrasonic echo input |
| GP5 | obstacle detection | ultrasonic trigger output |
| GP6 | | |
| GP7 | | |
| GP8 | motion control | motor 1 A, left wheel |
| GP9 | motion control | motor 1 B, left wheel |
| GP10 | motion control | motor 2 A, right wheel |
| GP11 | motion control | motor 2 B, right wheel |
| GP12 | | |
| GP13 | | |
| GP14 | | |
| GP15 | obstacle detection | scanning servo PWM |
| GP16 | odometry bring-up | left encoder A |
| GP17 | odometry bring-up | left encoder B |
| GP18 | odometry bring-up | right encoder A |
| GP19 | odometry bring-up / *example:* S1 | right encoder B / example LED; never enable both |
| GP20 | | |
| GP21 | | |
| GP22 | | |
| GP26 | line/barcode | right IR sensor, ADC0 |
| GP27 | line/barcode | center IR sensor, ADC1 |
| GP28 | line/barcode | left IR sensor, ADC2 |

These assignments record the supplied pin-connection table; they do not
enable any device or establish safe electrical levels. The table's ADC0 and
ADC2 labels were reversed relative to its GPIO numbers; the GPIO numbers were
confirmed. Verify sensor output voltages and calibration before activation.
The sensor used for barcode acquisition and the motor driver remain
unverified. No independent motor-disable arrangement is available.

## 3. Peripherals

**Subsystems, tasks and peripherals are three different things:**

| Term | What it is | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **Subsystem** | one student's part of the product, one folder | "environment sensing", "motor control", "user interface" |
| **Task** | one thread of execution. A subsystem may have **as many tasks as it needs** | a sampling task and a filtering task, both in "environment sensing" |
| **Peripheral** | one hardware block on the RP2040 | I2C0, UART1, the ADC |

A driver is **not** a subsystem. It is code *inside* the subsystem that
needs that hardware: the "environment sensing" subsystem contains the I2C
sensor code, and owns I2C0. Write it as `drv_<name>.c` / `drv_<name>.h` in
that subsystem's folder, never under `device/`, and call it only from the
peripheral's owning task. The kernel's own drivers in `device/` (serial,
I2C, ADC) may be used instead; switching one on in `config/config_device.h`
changes shared configuration, so record it in the table below first.

**The rule: each peripheral is driven by exactly one task, its *owning
task*.** That task can be in any subsystem, and one subsystem may own
several peripherals (one owning task each, or one task owning all of them).
Every other task, in the same subsystem or another one, asks the owning task
through a kernel object (section 5) and never touches that peripheral itself.

*Why:* this port runs tasks on both cores at the same time. If two tasks
drive the same I2C or UART block at once, their transfers get mixed up, and
the drivers have no locking to stop it.

| Peripheral | Owner subsystem | Owning task | Pins |
|---|---|---|---|
| UART1 | | | |
| I2C0 | | | |
| I2C1 | IMU | IMU task (planned) | GP2, GP3 |
| SPI0 | | | |
| SPI1 | | | |
| ADC | line/barcode | line/barcode task (planned) | GP26â€“GP28 |
| PWM slices 4, 5 | motion control | motion task (planned) | GP8â€“GP11 |
| PWM slice 7 | obstacle detection | obstacle task (planned) | GP15 |
| WiFi radio, TCP/IP, MQTT | *kernel* | `cyw43_utk_task` (built in) | GP23â€“25, GP29 |

I2C1 sharing with other devices is not yet confirmed. The kernel I2C driver
remains disabled; when enabled, startup initializes only I2C1 on GP2/GP3 as
`iicb`. I2C0 still has GP8/GP9 configured and must not be initialized without
reassigning its motor pins. Confirm bus ownership before enabling I2C. PWM
ownership covers the entire slice, including any unused channel on the same
slice.

### WiFi and MQTT are special

The radio and the network stack belong to the kernel's built-in WiFi service
task, `cyw43_utk_task` (`lib/libwifi/sysdepend/pico_rp2040/`). **No student
task may call `cyw43_...`, lwIP or MQTT functions, not even from a
"WiFi subsystem".** The calls are not safe from any other task, on either core.

What students *can* do:

- **Read the connection state** from any task, which is always safe:
  ```c
  #include "cyw43_utk.h"
  T_CYW43_UTK_STATUS st;
  cyw43_utk_get_status(&st);   /* st.ready, st.link_status, st.ip_addr, ... */
  ```
- **Send or receive data over the network** by adding code that *runs
  inside the service task*. The MQTT client is in
  `lib/liblwip/sysdepend/pico_rp2040/mqtt_utk.c`. It publishes a heartbeat
  today. A "cloud / reporting" subsystem would add a kernel object
  (e.g. a message buffer) that application tasks fill. The MQTT code then
  reads it with `TMO_POL` (never blocking) and publishes it.
  Because this changes shared library code, **agree it with the team and
  the instructor first**, and record it as an interface in section 5.
  It needs the `NET=lwip MQTT=1` build (README section 3).

## 4. Task priorities

1 is the highest. 1â€“4 are used by the kernel (USB console 3, WiFi 4). Each subsystem gets its own band by default;
change it only by team agreement. Write down every task you create.

| Subsystem | Priority band | Tasks (name â€“ priority â€“ core) |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | 12â€“13 | *example:* led_task â€“ 12 â€“ any |
| 2 | 14â€“15 | *example:* producer_task â€“ 14 â€“ core 1 |
| 3 | 16â€“17 | *example:* consumer_task â€“ 16 â€“ core 0 |
| 4 | 18â€“19 | *example:* monitor_task â€“ 18 â€“ any |
| 5 | 20â€“21 | |
| Vehicle controller | 10 | `vehicle_controller_task` â€“ 10 â€“ any (opt-in) |

Core: `TP_PRC1` = core 0, `TP_PRC2` = core 1, or "any".

## 5. Interfaces between subsystems

Every arrow between two subsystems is listed here. The **provider** creates
the kernel object and publishes its ID in its own `subsystemN.h`; the **user** only
sends to it or waits on it. Agree on the data format before coding.

| ID | From â†’ To | Mechanism | Object ID name | Data / meaning | Agreed by |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| IF-01 | S2 â†’ S3 | mailbox (owned by S3) | `mbx_samples` | `SAMPLE_MSG` in `subsystem2.h` | *example* |
| IF-02 | S3 â†’ S2 | semaphore + fixed memory pool (owned by S2) | `sem_slots`, `mpf_samples` | receiver returns the block, then 1 slot | *example* |
| IF-03 | S3 â†’ S4 | message buffer + event flag (owned by S4) | `mbf_summary`, `flg_events` | text line â‰¤ `S4_SUMMARY_MAX` bytes, then `FLG_BATCH` | *example* |
| IF-04 | S4 â†’ S3 | shared data under a mutex (owned by S3) | `app_stats`, `mtx_stats` | `APP_STATS`, read only while locked | *example* |
| IF-05 | S4 â†’ S1 | task control | `tid_led` | `tk_sus_tsk` / `tk_rsm_tsk` / `tk_chg_pri` | *example* |
| IF-06 | | | | | |
| IF-07 | application WiFi service â†’ built-in network owner | copied message buffer, nonblocking | bridge-owned, private | legacy five-byte telemetry on `robot/telemetry`, QoS 0; opt-in, no delivery guarantee | team and instructor agreement confirmed |
| IF-08 | built-in network owner â†’ application WiFi service | atomic link snapshot | bridge-owned, private | link and MQTT readiness with operating-clock timestamp; stale snapshots fault the service | team and instructor agreement confirmed |
| IF-09 | telemetry producer â†’ application WiFi service | copied message buffer, nonblocking | `g_mbf_telemetry` | ordered telemetry messages; producer handles full-buffer and other errors | pending |
| IF-10 | motion task â†’ controller | copied message buffer | `g_mbf_motion_status` | sequenced 100 ms commanded-PWM, active-command and latched-fault state; controller period â‰¤50 ms; no ARM acknowledgement or physical feedback | implementation draft; hardware validation pending |
| IF-11 | vehicle controller â†’ motion task | copied message buffer, nonblocking | `g_mbf_motion_commands` | ARM/STOP/emergency-stop; VELOCITY is not yet issued | implementation in progress |
| IF-12 | trusted local input provider â†’ vehicle controller | task-context callback | configured callback/context | GO and emergency-stop levels; callback is bounded and context remains alive for task lifetime | hardware binding pending |

Each interface is tested by at least one integration test
(`testing/integration/TEST_LOG.md`).

## 6. Kernel object budget

# Pico Car: Testing and Integration Plan

This document outlines the complete testing and integration strategy for your Raspberry Pi Pico W autonomous robot car system.

## System Architecture Overview

Your system consists of 8 major subsystems running on a dual-core RP2040 with micro T-Kernel 3.0:

1. **Line Detection & Barcode Reading** - ADC-based line sensing and barcode scanning
2. **Odometry** - Wheel encoder tracking for position estimation
3. **IMU Processing** - LSM303DLHC accelerometer/magnetometer for orientation
4. **Obstacle Avoidance** - HC-SR04 ultrasonic distance sensing
5. **Motion Control** - Motor drive and speed regulation via PID
6. **Vehicle Controller** - High-level steering and safety logic
7. **GPIO & Interrupt Routing** - Hardware event dispatch
8. **WiFi & MQTT** - Telemetry and remote control

Message flow is organized through centralized buffers with multiple policies (keep-latest, ordered, priority).

---

## Testing Levels and Strategy

### Level 1: Unit Tests (Individual Subsystems)

Each subsystem has its own unit tests run at startup when `TEST=1` is used.

**Key Components to Test:**

#### 1.1 Odometry Subsystem
- **Unit Tests (S2-U01 through S2-U0X)**
  - Encoder pulse counting accuracy
  - Odometry math (distance, angle calculations)
  - Two-encoder synchronization
  - Edge cases: zero counts, count wrapping, missed pulses
  - Producer-consumer interaction between encoder and odometry tasks
  
- **Test Code Location:** `app_program/odometry/odometry_test.c`, `odometry_producer_test.c`
- **Test Build:** `make TEST=1` includes these automatically
- **Hardware Needed:** Encoders with signals, or GPIO signal simulator

**Automated Checks:**
```
[TEST] ODO PASS         - odometry calculations
[TEST] ODO-PRODUCER PASS - producer tests
[TEST] ODO-TASK PASS    - task synchronization
```

---

#### 1.2 Line Detection & Barcode Subsystem
- **Unit Tests (S3-U01 through S3-U0X)**
  - ADC line reading and threshold detection
  - Line classification (on-line, left deviation, right deviation)
  - Barcode scanning and decoding
  - Intersection detection
  - Confidence metrics
  
- **Test Code Location:** `app_program/line_barcode/line_observation_test.c`, `barcode_scanner_test.c`
- **Hardware Needed:** Line sensor array (typically 8-16 ADC channels)

**Automated Checks:**
```
[TEST] LINE PASS       - line classification
[TEST] BAR PASS        - barcode decoder
[TEST] LB-TASK PASS    - line barcode task
[TEST] LINE-ADC PASS   - ADC reader
```

---

#### 1.3 IMU Subsystem
- **Unit Tests (S4-U01 through S4-U0X)**
  - LSM303DLHC I2C communication
  - Acceleration to roll/pitch conversion
  - Magnetometer to yaw conversion
  - Shock and tilt detection
  - Terrain classification
  - Calibration and zero-offset handling
  
- **Test Code Location:** `app_program/imu/imu_processor_test.c`, `imu_task_test.c`
- **Hardware Needed:** LSM303DLHC sensor on I2C bus
- **Host-Side Tests:** `testing/integration/imu_i2c_host/test.c` (runs on Linux/WSL without robot)

**Automated Checks:**
```
[TEST] IMU PASS       - IMU processing
[TEST] IMU-TASK PASS  - task tests
```

---

#### 1.4 Obstacle Avoidance Subsystem
- **Unit Tests (S5-U01 through S5-U0X)**
  - HC-SR04 ultrasonic pulse timing
  - Distance calculation from echo pulse width
  - Multi-sensor scanning (left, center, right, etc.)
  - Obstacle detection and classification
  - Emergency stop triggering
  - Command processing (scan, reset, abort)
  
- **Test Code Location:** `app_program/obstacle/obs_profile_test.c`, `obs_task_test.c`
- **Hardware Needed:** HC-SR04 ultrasonic sensors
- **Host-Side Tests:** `testing/integration/hcsr04_host/test.c` (distance conversion, runs without robot)

**Automated Checks:**
```
[TEST] OBS PASS      - obstacle profiling
[TEST] OBS-TASK PASS - task tests
```

---

#### 1.5 Motion Control Subsystem
- **Unit Tests (S6-U01 through S6-U0X)**
  - Motor PWM generation
  - Wheel speed feedback processing
  - PID controller tuning
  - Speed ramping and acceleration profiles
  - Stop/arm/velocity/turn modes
  - Motion status reporting
  - Fault detection (stalled motors, odometry mismatch)
  
- **Test Code Location:** `app_program/motion/wheel_speed_test.c`, `motion_task_test.c`
- **Hardware Needed:** Motors, motor driver, speed encoder feedback
- **Simulation:** `motion_task_test.c` includes simulated odometry for PID tuning

**Automated Checks:**
```
[TEST] MOT PASS      - wheel speed control
[TEST] MOT-TASK PASS - simulation tests
```

---

#### 1.6 Vehicle Controller Subsystem
- **Unit Tests (S7-U01 through S7-U0X)**
  - Line-following steering logic
  - Speed adaptation based on line confidence
  - Obstacle response (stop, turn, reverse)
  - Intersection handling
  - Barcode processing and navigation
  - Safety gates (speed limits, emergency stop)
  - State machine transitions
  
- **Test Code Location:** `app_program/controller/car_gate_test.c`, `line_steering_test.c`, `controller_monitor_test.c`
- **Simulation:** Most tests use simulated sensor data

**Automated Checks:**
```
[TEST] VC PASS        - controller gate and line steering
[TEST] VC-MONITOR PASS - monitor tests
[TEST] VC-TASK PASS   - vehicle controller task tests
```

---

#### 1.7 GPIO & Interrupt Routing
- **Unit Tests (S8-U01 through S8-U0X)**
  - GPIO interrupt dispatch
  - Pin assignment verification
  - Interrupt timing (latency from GPIO to handler)
  - Edge detection (rising, falling, both)
  
- **Test Code Location:** `app_program/gpio/gpio_irq_test.c`

**Automated Checks:**
```
[TEST] GPIO PASS - interrupt routing
```

---

#### 1.8 WiFi & MQTT
- **Unit Tests (S9-U01 through S9-U0X)**
  - WiFi connection to SSID
  - MQTT broker connection
  - Telemetry message formatting
  - Command reception and parsing
  - Network reconnection after loss
  - MQTT keep-alive / heartbeat
  
- **Test Code Location:** `app_program/wifi/wifi_service.c`, `wifi_mqtt_bridge.c`
- **Hardware Needed:** WiFi network access

**Automated Checks:**
```
[TEST] WIFI PASS        - service tests
[TEST] WIFI-BRIDGE PASS - bridge tests
```

---

### Level 2: Integration Tests (Between Subsystems)

Integration tests verify that message passing and subsystem interaction work correctly.

#### 2.1 Encoder â†’ Odometry Integration (INT-01)

**What:** Encoder pulses correctly feed into odometry calculation

**Test Case:**
1. Drive forward with known encoder counts
2. Verify odometry reports correct distance
3. Drive in circle, verify heading calculation

**Expected Result:** Distance within 5% of actual, heading within 5 degrees

**Manual Test Steps:**
- Mark start and end points (e.g., 1 meter)
- Drive forward
- Check reported distance against actual

**Automation:** `testing/integration/encoder_odometry/`
- Need encoder pulse generator or simulator
- Verify message buffer `VEHICLE_BUFFER_WHEEL_ODOMETRY` receives correct values

---

#### 2.2 Line Sensor â†’ Line Observation Integration (INT-02)

**What:** ADC readings correctly map to line position and confidence

**Test Case:**
1. Position sensor over line
2. Verify line_detected flag = 1
3. Move sensor left/right
4. Verify lateral_error and confidence values

**Expected Result:**
- On line: lateral_error â‰ˆ 0, confidence â‰ˆ 255
- Off line: line_detected = 0
- Linearity: lateral_error scales with position

**Automation:**
- Mock ADC values with known patterns
- Verify `VEHICLE_BUFFER_LINE_OBSERVATIONS` messages

---

#### 2.3 IMU â†’ Orientation Integration (INT-03)

**What:** I2C IMU data correctly converts to roll/pitch/yaw estimates

**Test Case:**
1. Tilt robot 45 degrees
2. Verify roll_mdeg â‰ˆ 45000 (millidegrees)
3. Rotate horizontally
4. Verify yaw_mdeg changes

**Expected Result:**
- Roll/pitch within Â±10 degrees at 45-degree tilt
- Yaw rotates smoothly, no jumps > 10 degrees

**Host-Side Unit Test (runs on Linux/WSL):**
```sh
cd testing/integration/imu_i2c_host
gcc -std=c11 -Wall -Wextra -Werror -Isys -I../../../app_program/imu \
  test.c ../../../app_program/imu/lsm303dlhc_i2c.c \
  ../../../app_program/imu/lsm303dlhc_reader.c \
  ../../../app_program/imu/lsm303dlhc_accel.c -o imu_i2c_host_test
./imu_i2c_host_test
```

**Robot Test:**
- Mount robot on level surface
- Run IMU task, verify zero roll/pitch
- Tilt and verify angle reporting

---

#### 2.4 Obstacle Sensor â†’ Obstacle Event Integration (INT-04)

**What:** HC-SR04 echo pulses correctly convert to distance and obstacle events

**Test Case:**
1. Place object 20cm from sensor
2. Verify distance_mm â‰ˆ 200
3. Move object away
4. Verify distance increases linearly

**Expected Result:**
- Distance within Â±2cm of actual
- No false detections in open air

**Host-Side Unit Test:**
```sh
cd testing/integration/hcsr04_host
gcc -std=c11 -Wall -Wextra -Werror -I../../../app_program/obstacle \
  test.c ../../../app_program/obstacle/hcsr04.c \
  ../../../app_program/obstacle/hcsr04_capture.c -o hcsr04_host_test
./hcsr04_host_test
```

**Robot Test:**
- Verify at multiple distances (15cm, 30cm, 50cm, 100cm)
- Check for timeout handling with no object

---

#### 2.5 Odometry + Controller â†’ Motion Integration (INT-05)

**What:** Motion commands correctly execute based on controller output

**Test Case:**
1. Command VELOCITY mode with linear_velocity = 50 mm/s
2. Verify motor PWM output
3. Measure actual wheel speed via encoder feedback
4. Verify speed-to-PWM mapping accuracy

**Expected Result:**
- Requested speed within 10% of actual
- No oscillation in speed (PID stability)

**Automation:**
- Monitor `VEHICLE_BUFFER_MOTION_STATUS` for actual motor PWM
- Compare against `VEHICLE_BUFFER_MOTION_COMMANDS`

---

#### 2.6 Controller + Obstacle â†’ Safety Gate Integration (INT-06)

**What:** Obstacle detection correctly halts motion via safety gate

**Test Case:**
1. Robot moving forward at 100 mm/s
2. Introduce obstacle at 20cm
3. Verify robot stops immediately
4. Check emergency stop code is set

**Expected Result:**
- Stop within 100ms of obstacle detection
- motion_status shows emergency stop

**Manual Test:**
- Drive robot toward wall
- Measure stop distance (< 15cm from wall)

---

#### 2.7 Line + Barcode â†’ Navigation Integration (INT-07)

**What:** Barcode events correctly trigger navigation waypoints

**Test Case:**
1. Print barcodes on track (start, checkpoint, end)
2. Robot follows line and detects barcodes
3. Verify each barcode event is logged
4. Check navigation state changes appropriately

**Expected Result:**
- All barcodes detected in sequence
- No false barcode detections

**Manual Test:**
- Create test track with printed barcodes
- Drive robot, log all barcode events
- Verify sequence matches expected

---

#### 2.8 Telemetry â†’ WiFi Integration (INT-08)

**What:** Motion/sensor data correctly publishes to MQTT

**Test Case:**
1. Robot connected to WiFi
2. MQTT broker receiving heartbeat
3. Move robot or trigger sensor events
4. Verify telemetry messages appear in MQTT

**Expected Result:**
- Heartbeat every 10 seconds
- All sensor data published with < 500ms latency

**Manual Test:**
- Use MQTT client (e.g., `mosquitto_sub` or MQTT.fx) to subscribe to robot topic
- Verify messages appear

---

### Level 3: System Tests (End-to-End Product Requirements)

System tests verify the robot meets its original requirements.

#### 3.1 Line Following (SYS-01)

**Requirement:** Robot tracks a black line on white background

**Test Setup:**
- Print or tape a line track on floor (straight sections, curves, intersections)
- Mark start and finish

**Test Steps:**
1. Place robot on start
2. Command line-follow mode
3. Robot autonomously follows line to finish
4. Record time and completion distance

**Success Criteria:**
- Reaches finish without leaving line
- Time within acceptable threshold (e.g., < 60 seconds for 3-meter track)
- No collisions with obstacles

**Evidence:** Video showing full run

---

#### 3.2 Barcode Navigation (SYS-02)

**Requirement:** Robot detects barcodes and executes waypoint navigation

**Test Setup:**
- Print/tape barcodes at 3-5 waypoints along line
- Each barcode encodes a target speed or action

**Test Steps:**
1. Robot starts with barcode "START"
2. Robot reads each barcode
3. Robot adapts speed or direction per barcode
4. Robot ends at barcode "END"

**Success Criteria:**
- All barcodes read correctly
- Navigation matches expected sequence
- Robot completes course

**Evidence:** Console log showing barcode detections, video of run

---

#### 3.3 Obstacle Avoidance (SYS-03)

**Requirement:** Robot detects obstacles and takes evasive action

**Test Setup:**
- Line track with obstacles (boxes, cones) placed ahead at 20cm, 50cm, 100cm

**Test Steps:**
1. Robot approaches obstacle
2. Robot detects at target distance
3. Robot slows/stops
4. Robot steers around (optional) or waits
5. Robot resumes after obstacle passes

**Success Criteria:**
- Obstacle detected within Â±5cm of setpoint distance
- Robot stops before collision
- Can resume after obstacle removed

**Evidence:** Video showing obstacle detection and response, console logs

---

#### 3.4 Heading Stability (SYS-04)

**Requirement:** Robot maintains heading despite obstacles and sensor noise

**Test Setup:**
- Flat, open area; no line, no obstacles

**Test Steps:**
1. Robot armed, stationary on level surface
2. Heading recorded via IMU
3. Robot driven forward in straight line
4. Heading monitored continuously
5. Verify minimal yaw drift

**Success Criteria:**
- Yaw drift < 5 degrees over 2-meter distance
- No oscillation in heading

**Evidence:** Logged IMU data, video showing straight path

---

#### 3.5 Telemetry & Remote Control (SYS-05)

**Requirement:** Robot connects to network and accepts remote commands

**Test Setup:**
- Robot and MQTT broker on same network
- Remote control client (dashboard or CLI MQTT publisher)

**Test Steps:**
1. Robot powers on and connects to WiFi
2. Telemetry starts publishing (heartbeat, sensor data)
3. Send VELOCITY command via MQTT
4. Robot executes command
5. Send STOP command
6. Robot stops

**Success Criteria:**
- WiFi connection successful within 10 seconds
- MQTT messages appear with < 500ms latency
- Commands execute within 100ms
- Robot responds to STOP immediately

**Evidence:** Console logs, MQTT client screenshots

---

#### 3.6 Dual-Core Performance (SYS-06)

**Requirement:** System operates stably on dual-core RP2040

**Test Setup:**
- Enable SMP (dual-core) build: `make SMP=1`
- Stress-test with all subsystems active

**Test Steps:**
1. Build and flash SMP firmware
2. Run for 5 minutes with continuous operation
3. Monitor CPU load and task scheduling
4. Check for deadlocks or race conditions

**Success Criteria:**
- No system hangs or crashes
- All tasks meet their deadlines
- Message buffers do not overflow

**Evidence:** Console output showing "dual-core" message, uptime

---

#### 3.7 Power & Startup (SYS-07)

**Requirement:** Robot powers on reliably and reaches operational state

**Test Setup:**
- Robot on bench, USB power only
- Measure startup time to first autonomous action

**Test Steps:**
1. Power on (USB or battery)
2. Count time to first motor movement or sensor reading
3. Verify all LEDs illuminate
4. Check console output for any errors

**Success Criteria:**
- Boot time < 5 seconds
- All subsystems initialize without errors
- Ready for commands

**Evidence:** Console log showing boot messages

---

#### 3.8 Long-Duration Run (SYS-08)

**Requirement:** Robot operates continuously for extended period (e.g., 1 hour)

**Test Setup:**
- Line track, obstacles, and waypoints for realistic operation

**Test Steps:**
1. Robot runs autonomously on line-following task
2. Monitor for 1 hour continuously
3. Log any errors or anomalies
4. Verify telemetry publishing throughout

**Success Criteria:**
- No crashes or hangs
- Consistent sensor readings
- Memory not leaking
- All telemetry messages published

**Evidence:** Console logs from entire run, uptime counter

---

## Testing Checklist

### Pre-Flight Checks (Before Each Test)

- [ ] Pico W powered and USB serial connected
- [ ] WiFi SSID and password configured in `config/wifi_secrets.h`
- [ ] MQTT broker IP and credentials correct
- [ ] All sensors connected and powered
- [ ] Motors and motor driver functioning
- [ ] Encoders reading pulses
- [ ] Console output visible (115200 baud, 8N1)

### Build & Flash Commands

```bash
# Build unit tests only
cd build_make
make TEST=1 -j8

# Build unit tests + template examples
make TEST=1 EXAMPLES=1 -j8

# Build release (no tests)
make -j8

# Single-core build (for comparison)
make TEST=1 SMP=0 -j8

# Flash the .uf2 file
# Hold BOOTSEL button, plug in USB, release button
# Drag mtk3pico_smp1_usb_cdc_wifi.uf2 to RPI-RP2 mass storage device
```

### Serial Console Capture

```bash
# macOS
cat /dev/cu.usbmodem* | tee testing/subsystemN/logs/2026-11-13_test.txt

# Linux / WSL
cat /dev/ttyACM0 | tee testing/subsystemN/logs/2026-11-13_test.txt
```

### Test Execution Workflow

1. **Define test** in appropriate `TEST_LOG.md` (subsystem or integration)
2. **Add automated check** if possible to `*_test.c`
3. **Build** with `make TEST=1`
4. **Flash** .uf2 to Pico
5. **Capture** console output to `logs/YYYY-MM-DD_<description>.txt`
6. **Record** result in `TEST_LOG.md`: date, ID, commit hash, tester, PASS/FAIL, log file
7. **Commit** both test log and console output file together

### Test Log Format

```markdown
## Test Cases

| ID | Test | How | Expected |
|---|---|---|---|
| INT-01 | Encoder â†’ odometry | Drive 1m, verify distance | Distance Â±5% |

## Results

| Date | Test | Commit | Tester | Result | Log |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2026-11-13 | INT-01 | abc1234 | @user | PASS | logs/2026-11-13_encoder_odometry.txt |
```

---

## Continuous Integration & Regression Testing

### Per-PR Checklist

Before merging any PR:
1. Run all unit tests (`make TEST=1`)
2. Verify no new test failures
3. If adding new subsystem, add unit tests to `*_test.c`
4. Update `TEST_LOG.md` with results
5. Capture and commit console logs

### Before Demo or Hand-In

Run complete system test suite:
```bash
# 1. Unit tests
make TEST=1 EXAMPLES=1 -j8
# Power cycle Pico, capture output

# 2. Host-side integration tests
cd testing/integration/i2c_host && gcc ... && ./test
cd testing/integration/imu_i2c_host && gcc ... && ./test
cd testing/integration/hcsr04_host && gcc ... && ./test

# 3. System tests (manual)
# Line following: drive track, record time
# Obstacle avoidance: place objects, verify stops
# Telemetry: verify MQTT messages
# Long-run: 30-60 minute autonomous operation
```

---

## Known Limitations & Workarounds

### 1. Host-Side Tests Do NOT Verify

- Actual I2C timing or bus timing
- Real sensor hardware
- GPIO interrupt latency
- Power consumption
- Temperature effects

**Workaround:** Always run final tests on actual robot hardware.

### 2. Simulated Odometry in Motion Tests

Motion controller tests (`motion_task_test`) use simulated encoder feedback, not real wheel motion.

**Workaround:** Run motion integration tests (INT-05) on robot with real motors and encoders.

### 3. WiFi Only Available on Pico W

Standard Pico (non-W) cannot run WiFi tests. WiFi tests (SYS-05) require Pico W.

---

## Troubleshooting

### Test Hangs or Timeout

**Problem:** Test runs but never completes

**Steps:**
1. Check console output for [TEST] messages
2. Verify Pico has power and is not in reset loop
3. Recompile with `make clean` and rebuild
4. Check if task is blocked in infinite loop (search code for `for(;;)`)

---

### Test Crashes (Prints ERROR or Fault)

**Problem:** Test starts but system crashes with error code

**Steps:**
1. Note the error code and subsystem
2. Check `app_program/subsystemN/subsystemN_test.c` for the test
3. Add `tm_printf` debug statements to isolate failure point
4. Verify hardware connections
5. Check for stack overflow: increase `TASK_STACK_SIZE` if needed

---

### Incorrect Sensor Readings

**Problem:** Sensor values make no sense (e.g., distance = 0xFFFF)

**Steps:**
1. Verify sensor powered and connected
2. Check I2C/ADC configuration in `config/`
3. Run integration test to verify communication
4. Measure voltage at sensor pins with multimeter
5. Swap sensor to verify hardware not faulty

---

### Memory Leaks or Slowdown Over Time

**Problem:** System works fine for a while, then slows down or crashes

**Steps:**
1. Check console for message buffer overflow warnings
2. Verify no infinite loops in tasks
3. Check for large stack allocations that could fragment memory
4. Run SYS-08 (long-duration test) to reproduce
5. Add heap size monitoring (`tk_ref_mpf` calls)

---

## Next Steps

1. **Start with Unit Tests:** Run `make TEST=1` and verify all [TEST] messages show PASS
2. **Run Host-Side Integration Tests:** Test IMU and obstacle sensor on development machine
3. **Test on Robot Hardware:** Encoder â†’ odometry, line sensor, IMU tilt detection
4. **System Integration:** Obstacle avoidance, line following with controller
5. **End-to-End Validation:** Full autonomous runs with all subsystems active

# Testing & Integration Summary - Pico Car Project

## What You Have

A comprehensive autonomous robot car system with 8 subsystems:
- **Odometry:** Wheel encoder tracking
- **Line Detection & Barcode:** ADC-based line following with barcode waypoints
- **IMU:** Orientation and shock detection
- **Obstacle Avoidance:** Ultrasonic range sensing
- **Motion Control:** Motor PWM and speed PID
- **Vehicle Controller:** High-level steering and safety logic
- **GPIO/Interrupts:** Hardware event routing
- **WiFi/MQTT:** Network telemetry and remote commands

All running on **dual-core Raspberry Pi Pico W** with **micro T-Kernel 3.0** RTOS.

## Common Issues & Solutions

### Problem: Tests hang or crash
**Solution:** Check HARDWARE_INTEGRATION_GUIDE for GPIO/I2C/power issues

### Problem: Sensor reads garbage values
**Solution:** Verify pin connections, check voltage with multimeter

### Problem: Motor won't move
**Solution:** Check motor power supply (separate 6-12V), verify motor driver IC

### Problem: WiFi won't connect
**Solution:** Verify SSID/password in config/wifi_secrets.h, check 2.4GHz network

### Problem: Odometry distance is wrong
**Solution:** Calibrate wheel circumference, verify encoder pulses per rotation

### Problem: Tests take too long
**Solution:** Some tests run every subsystem; filter with smaller make targets

---

|---|---|
| Unit Tests | All [TEST] PASS, no ERROR messages |
| Integration Tests | 6/8 integration tests passing |
| System Tests | Autonomous line following works, 5+ min uptime |
| Deployment | 8/8 system tests passing, git history clean |
